THR Visionary: 5 Janusz Kaminski Shots

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The cinematographer, this year’s THR Visionary Award honoree, always lets film follow function, choosing a visual style to match the story being told.

Try to find a signature Janusz Kaminski “look” and you’ll come up empty. Yes, he likes backlighting and has a penchant for smoke and film-noir style. But the Polish-born cameraman, winner of the 2026 THR Visionary Award for excellence in cinematography, has spent three decades making images that bend to the story rather than the other way around.

Kaminski’s guiding principle is that form follows function. With lenses, film stocks, color palettes and camera movement chosen to serve what each film needs to tell its story. The result is a body of work remarkable for its originality and visual diversity. Here are five of Kaminski’s most original visions. Related Stories Movies Janusz Kaminski, the Polish Cameraman Who Taught Hollywood How to See Movies To Make a Doc About a Nomadic Cyclist, the 'Perfect Road' Filmmakers Spent 3 Months in a Campervan

Steven Spielberg’s Holocaust drama was shot in stark black-and-white. The choice wasn’t nostalgic but a way of conjuring newsreels and documentary record, with a handheld immediacy that keeps the audience in the room with the horror. Kaminski’s hard, contrasty lighting, harsh shadows and drifting smoke owe a debt to German Expressionism and postwar European cinema. Against this monochrome, the lone splash of color, the little girl’s red coat, lands as one of the most devastating images in modern film. It won him his first Oscar.

For the Omaha Beach landing and the war that follows, Kaminski set out to make combat look like frontline footage rather than a Hollywood spectacle. Colors are leached out to a washed, metallic palette. He stripped protective coatings from his lenses to invite flare and a rawer, less polished image, and used a narrow shutter angle that makes explosions and bullet strikes snap with a jittery, staccato clarity. Handheld cameras and spattered lenses put the viewer on the sand with the soldiers. The look was so influential that it reshaped how war was filmed for a generation, and it earned him a second Oscar.

In Julian Schnabel’s adaptation of Jean-Dominique Bauby’s memoir, written by a man paralyzed but for one eyelid, the camera becomes that eye. Kaminski opens the film from Bauby’s first-person perspective: blurred focus, tilted frames, sudden blinks and a claustrophobic narrowness of vision that gradually widens. As Bauby’s imagination takes flight, so does the imagery. Soft, luminous pastels and sunlit memories of Paris and the seaside offer escape from the hospital room. It’s an exercise in subjective cinematography that earned Kaminski an Oscar nomination.

If Saving Private Ryan rejected glossy spectacle, War Horse embraced it on purpose. Spielberg and Kaminski drew on the sweeping, painterly look of classic Hollywood Westerns and epics, with John Ford and David Lean in mind. Anamorphic widescreen frames showcase burnished golds, glowing skies and silhouetted figures against vast landscapes, giving the First World War tale a storybook romanticism before the mud and horror close in. It’s lush, deliberately old-fashioned and entirely suited to a story told through the eyes of a horse. It was another Oscar-nominated turn.

For Spielberg’s tense thriller about the aftermath of the 1972 Olympics massacre, Kaminski looked to the gritty, paranoid cinema of the 1970s. The film has a grainy, slightly faded texture with muted, earthy colors, and frequently uses zoom lenses, a hallmark of the era’s thrillers, to create a sense of surveillance and unease. The images feel period-authentic without becoming pastiche, and their rough-edged restraint keeps the focus on the moral toll of the mission.

Andy Richter Claims U2’s the Edge Wrote an ‘Elf’ Sequel Screenplay

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Andy Richter, who had a small role in the 2003 Christmas classic Elf, revealed in a new interview that U2’s the Edge wrote a screenplay for the sequel to the Will Ferrell-starring film.

For over two decades, Elf fans have clamored for a sequel to the film, and while an Elf 2 came close to happening in 2013, Ferrell ultimately decided against reprising the role of Buddy.

At some point after the film’s release and rise to holiday favorite, as Richter told Obsessed: The Podcast, Elf producer Shauna Robertson received a phone call from an unlikely screenwriter pitching a meeting about Elf 2.

“Somebody reached out to her and said, ‘The Edge from U2 has written a sequel to Elf, and he wants to talk to you about it,’” Richter said. “It’s the weirdest story, but apparently it was fully written somewhere in the world. There is an Edge-penned Elf 2 out there.”

Richter added that the meeting “never happened.” “It’s one of those things where she’s like, ‘All right, sure, I’ll meet him,’ and then it just petered out and never happened,” Richter said. Trending Stories Good Riddance to the Cybertruck, the Chariot of the Dumbest Gods 'SNL': Druski Impersonates 'Crazy' Elizabeth Holmes in Meta-Documentary 'SNL' Weekend Update Drags Trump Over Midterm Drubbing, Stephen Miller Promotion 'SNL' Cold Open: James Talarico Crashes Trump's Midterm Postmortem

While the Edge has hundreds of credits on his IMDB page thanks to his work with U2, “Screenwriter” is not among “Composer,” “Soundtrack,” and “Actor” (as himself) in his filmography. However, the Edge has served as a producer on a handful of occasions on non-U2 projects, including documentaries and short films.

The Edge has not commented, ever, on his Elf 2 script. U2, however, will gives fans an early Christmas present next week with the arrival of their new album Carnaval de Luz.

DC’s ‘Clayface’ Passes $100 Million After Being Officially Removed From 532 Theaters

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

He has covered everything from Marvel to the Oscars, and Marvel at the Oscars. He also writes obsessively about the box office, charting the many hits and misses that are released weekly, and how their commercial performance shapes public perception. In his time at Collider, he has also helped drive diversity by writing stories about the multiple Indian film industries, with a goal of introducing audiences to a whole new world of cinema.

Two weeks into its theatrical run, DC's Clayface has passed a coveted global box-office milestone. In doing so, the movie has also broken even theatrically — a rarity in this day and age, especially in the superhero genre. That said, the movie's comparatively smaller scale means that its actual box-office performance can't really hold a candle to some of the genre's bigger hits. However, Clayface remains on track to overtake this year's Supergirl, which ended its run with around $125 million worldwide and cost nearly four times as much.

Clayface was produced on a reported budget of $40 million — lower than some of the costlier horror movies we've seen this year. For instance, this year's Scream 7 cost $45 million to produce, while Resident Evil cost $75 million. On the other hand, $40 million is a king's ransom when compared to the $10 million production cost of Backrooms and the $1 million it cost to produce Obsession. Both those films were anomalies that played off each other to gross nearly $1 billion worldwide. In fact, 2019's Joker set the template for Clayface, grossing $1 billion against a reported budget of $55 million.

Joker, however, was also an anomaly. Clayface is playing like a regular horror movie and not an installment of a major superhero franchise. The movie has overtaken director James Watkins' previous film, Speak No Evil, which generated $77 million worldwide against a reported budget of $15 million, and should soon overtake the filmmaker's 2012 release The Woman in Black, which grossed around $130 million against a reported budget of $15 million.

Four villains, and four arguments the films were honest enough to actually let them make. Here is the one you lean towards, the part of it that holds up, and the exact point where it stops holding up.

You align with the one who was right about the problem and wrong about the solution. Killmonger is the most persuasive villain Marvel has produced because the film never pretends his anger came from nowhere.

You align with the protector, which is the most understandable and most dangerous position on this list. Namor is not chasing power or revenge, he is doing whatever keeps his people hidden, and he has been doing it for centuries.

You align with the patient one, the only man on this list with no powers, no army and no hurry. Zemo lost his family in Sokovia and then spent years working out that you do not fight the Avengers, you introduce them to each other's secrets.

You align with the one who decided the answer was arithmetic. Thanos is not angry and not grieving in any way he will admit to, he simply reached a conclusion and never once revisited it, which is precisely what makes him frightening.

Clayface lost more than 530 theaters as it entered its third week of release, having debuted in 3,458 theaters two weeks ago. The film added around $7 million this weekend, pushing its domestic total to nearly $60 million. Globally, Clayface has surpassed the $100 million milestone, overtaking Lee Cronin's The Mummy and Evil Dead Burn.

Starring Tom Rhys Harries, Naomi Ackie, Max Minghella, and Eddie Marsan, the film exists in an alternate Gotham City from the one seen in Matt Reeves' The Batman. This opens the door for DC Studios to make more cost-effective movies focusing on Batman's rogues' gallery. Clayface received positive reviews from critics and audiences alike, and is now sitting at a "Certified Fresh" 78% score on Rotten Tomatoes. The aggregator website's consensus reads, "Carving out a unique identity for itself in the DCU while closely resembling a litany of body horror classics, Clayface takes time to get going until it eventually morphs into an icky and satisfying revenge thriller." This is how Enzo Maresca's tactics have left Elliot Anderson exposed with too much to do, writes JACK GAUGHAN... and why £125m Enzo Fernandez must step up and offer much more to solve midfield riddle

📅 2026-11-08  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Published: 07:00 EDT, 8 November 2026 | Updated: 11:52 EDT, 8 November 2026

Manchester City spent £320million on overhauling their midfield over the summer. In doing so, they twice broke their own transfer record. This marked the most aggressive focus on a specific need since the lavish outlay on full backs nine years ago.

All signed weeks before the independent commission’s guilty verdicts were announced, Elliot Anderson, Enzo Fernandez and Ayyoub Bouaddi heralded a new era in the City engine room. More energy, vibrancy, dynamism.

Two of them were ready-made replacements for Rodri and Bernardo Silva, with Bouaddi only 18 at the time and purchased as a potential superstar after shooting to global prominence at the World Cup.

But as Enzo Maresca heads into the second international break of the season on the back of three consecutive Premier League defeats, his midfield is the primary area of concern. And has been for a while.

A big fortnight lies in store for City’s manager, whose midfield has been wide open all year. Fine when outscoring opponents – see the 5-3 victory over Sunderland – and it makes for great entertainment yet an obvious gaping chasm when the goals dry up slightly.

The balance has not felt right and Maresca, who has gently batted away persistent questions about the midfield for weeks, must know this.

Elliot Anderson is being asked to do too much and it cost City in the 3-1 defeat at Forest

While studying tapes of all three defeats over the coming days, before City come back after the internationals to host Fulham and then go to Arsenal, he needs to determine a plan moving forward and that will include some tough decisions on individuals and shapes.

Nottingham Forest could have driven a bus through the middle of the park on Saturday night, two goals from counter-attacking situations engineered between the width of the centre circle. Twice the ostensible holding midfielder Anderson tried to nick possession, beaten to it, and City were turned around.

Noises coming from the dressing room suggest that Anderson is effectively being asked to operate as both a No6 and No8 when picked as the deepest of the three, to go and join play high up the pitch. That is Maresca leaning into the England international’s Action Man, everything to everyone style. That also relies on somebody covering for him.

That somebody does not exist in this current setup, Fernandez always too high and wide elsewhere.

So when they lose duels as happened at the City Ground, something the manager openly bemoaned, City are not stable enough to recover.

Liam Delap's fierce finish put Forest 2-0 ahead - and City were left playing catch-up

Club analysts have sat down with Anderson to go over video clips on how best to follow these instructions to the benefit of the team. Watching him, it’s hard not to conclude that this is a quality midfielder being asked to do too much as a single deep midfielder.

England are sweating on the fitness of Marc Guehi as the Manchester City defender battles a hip injury.

Guehi came off at half time of Saturday's 3-1 defeat by Nottingham Forest, with Thomas Tuchel's squad set to link up in London this morning.

With John Stones already out of Nations League ties with Croatia and Spain, Tuchel would be severely light on experience at centre half should Guehi not prove his fitness.

It will produce moments of sheer class – that lung-busting run up the right in Leipzig on Wednesday that bore hallmarks of Kevin De Bruyne, a perfect cross for Erling Haaland’s winner – while also leaving the defence exposed.

An exposed defence lends itself to a heightened chance of individual errors, plenty of those around at the moment, and it was noticeable at Forest that the opening goal came from uncertainty in possession. Josko Gvardiol made the error, eased off the ball by eventual goalscorer Jair Cunha, but the plan cannot be to have the left back with his back to goal and no viable passing options.

When Marc Guehi’s ball went into Gvardiol, Anderson was higher and wider on the right – deep in conversation with Abdukodir Khusanov about how they’d failed to move possession well enough earlier in the sequence – and Rayan Cherki was belatedly trotting back to offer himself.

Fernandez never once enters the frame, even when Cunha is in the box and subsequently wheeling away in celebration. Maresca rejected the suggestion that there is an issue with the team’s build-up on Saturday night.

As Man City look to build from the back in the third minute, Anderson (No 5) is busy delivering instructions to Khusanov (out of shot)...

... it means that when Guehi plays the ball to Gvardiol, he has his back to goal and nobody in midfield to pass to - and is already under pressure

... and with no support, three Forest players swarm and Jair Cunha dispossesses him, leading to him scoring the opening goal

What is clear though is that there is not yet that balance, that this is not yet a unit. Maresca moved to include Bouaddi with Anderson and Fernandez against Brighton – a one-goal defeat – but that acted as detrimental to creativity. If nothing else, Brighton proved that City require either Cherki or Phil Foden on the pitch at all times.

Over the past two games, Fernandez has lurked more menacingly at the back post, offering an additional goal threat rather than just relying on Haaland. That ghosting late run saw him score in Leipzig but for a joint-British record £125million needs to offer so much more. There will be growing calls for Bouaddi’s inclusion as a genuine deep-lying midfielder but it’s unlikely that the teenager can manage the league’s physicality as a single pivot.

The Moroccan has more chance of stamping his authority when alongside somebody else, dovetailing with an Anderson as a two. That leaves Fernandez, whose barnstorming league debut at Old Trafford feels like a long time ago now.

To persist with him, he either dislodges Cherki as the No10 on the promise of far more goals or Maresca drops him back into a more conventional role that allows Anderson to flourish properly.

Something has to give because the unevenness of City’s three is giving the rest of the team uncomfortable jitters.

By far the most successful movie star in the world at the time, Harrison Ford took a big swing with a 1985 film that has since emerged as a cult classic. The movie was a solid sleeper hit at the box office, having received excellent reviews and earned Ford a Best Actor nomination at the Oscars. It was, however, unlike the more populist entertainment that he was best known for. Now, more than four decades after its initial theatrical run, the movie in question has been added to the library of a free streaming platform.

The film was directed by Peter Weir, who broke out with the Australian psychological thriller Picnic at Hanging Rock, and went on to direct classics such as The Truman Show and Master and Commander: The Far Side of the World. The film with Ford, however, was neither a fantasy fable nor a soaring action epic. It was a neo-noir mystery about a tortured detective protecting a young mother and her son amid a murder investigation in the Amish community.

The movie also featured a young Lukas Haas and Kelly McGillis, who, only a year later, would star alongside Tom Cruise in Top Gun. Interestingly, the action classic was also added to the same free streamer this month. Unlike Top Gun, however, the neo-noir mystery wasn't a record-setting blockbuster. Instead, its trajectory at the box office was more gradual. That said, it went on to gross around $120 million worldwide against a reported budget of $12 million.

We're talking, of course, about Witness. The film was nominated for eight Oscars, including Best Picture. It ended up winning in the Best Original Screenplay category. Witness now holds a "Certified Fresh" 91% score on the aggregator website Rotten Tomatoes, where the consensus reads, "A wonderfully entertaining thriller within an unusual setting, with Harrison Ford delivering a surprisingly emotive and sympathetic performance."

You have never explained yourself and you have never needed to, because people work out fairly quickly that you are the most capable person in the room without you ever having to mention it.

You are the funniest person in most rooms and the most loyal person in all of them, and you would be genuinely insulted if somebody suggested those two things were in any tension.

You never wanted this job, and you took it anyway because the alternative was standing there watching somebody less careful do it badly, which is the most Western reason there has ever been.

‘Rogue One’s Best Scene Was Actually the Worst Thing to Ever Happen To Star Wars

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Julio is a Senior Author for Collider. He studied History and International Relations at university, but found his calling in cultural journalism. When he isn't writing, Julio also teaches English at a nearby school. He has lived in São Paulo most of his life, where he covers CCXP and other big events. Having loved movies, music, and TV from an early age, he prides himself in knowing every minute detail about the things he loves. When he is older, he dreams of owning a movie theater in a small countryside town.

It was hard to contain the excitement viewers felt after walking out of Rogue One: A Star Wars Story when it first premiered in 2016 — especially after that ending. The movie was an undeniable game changer for Star Wars, and Darth Vader's hallway carnage scene felt like the crowning achievement. It's incredible, indeed, and it belongs in Rogue One right at that moment. Ten years later, it remains one of the franchise's finest hours, but it's also impossible to ignore how that hallway scene impacted not just the galaxy far, far away, but the fandom, too. From how stories are conceived to fan expectations, the overall effect of Vader's hallway scene has unfortunately not been as positive as one might expect.

It's undeniable that the hallway scene is one of the coolest things about Rogue One, and a top Darth Vader moment. The way he casually walks into that hallway, lets his mechanical breath fill the room, and ignites his lightsaber with perfect timing highlights how much of a drama queen he really is, and it's all for the benefit of the scene, really. It even started a franchise trend. However, all that goes so hard, some fans forget (or ignore?) that the scene's not really about him; it's about the rebels.

The purpose of the movie is to tell the story of how the rebels got the Death Star plans, using the Rogue One team to illustrate how much the Rebellion had to sacrifice to achieve that small, preliminary victory. Vader's role is to embody the abysmal difference between the Empire and the Rebellion's capabilities, in that sense, and that's what the hallway carnage is all about: even after all that, the Empire still has a literal Sith Lord to do what its whole army and navy couldn't.

The moment really was vital to Rogue One's story. It's supposed to highlight the constant sense of urgency inherent to being a rebel, because they are always much closer to defeat than to victory, regardless of how much they sacrifice and how righteous their cause is. It's also something that we all knew Vader could do, but never actually saw; such a moment could never fit within the Original Trilogy, for example. Unfortunately, what some viewers took away from the scene was a distinctly opposite impression.

It didn't take long for Vader's hallway scene to have its meaning twisted, however, with certain parts of the fandom seeing in it as something completely different than intended. The argument that it symbolizes what Star Wars could and should be has been repeated ever since: grittier and more adult (really meaning "violent").

Pretty much every franchise release after that has been divisive, regardless of purpose or whether they fit within these expectations. Vader was brought back in Obi-Wan Kenobi, just as vicious and even more important, but it was overlooked. The Acolyte appealed to Prequel Trilogy apologists and featured an equally awesome villain with The Stranger (Manny Jacinto), but, then, it fell to discourse. Even Andor, a direct prequel to Rogue One and the grittiest and most realistic work in Star Wars, wasn't enough — there was no Vader.

None of those are perfect, of course, but the fact remains that, after that hallway scene, fanboy discourse robbed Star Wars of its right to simply be fun. Some people saw it as an outlet for their worst opinions while ignoring what the scene really means, and with their sheer volume were loud enough to influence other parts of the fandom to agree with them. None of that is the scene's own fault, of course, but it's time these things were properly faced.

As Mon Mothma (Genevieve O'Reilly) brilliantly says in Andor Season 2, ignoring the truth means becoming "vulnerable to the appetite of whatever monster screams the loudest." The truth here is that Star Wars is in worse shape now than it was before Rogue One — not because of the quality of its products, but because it let itself be affected by those screaming loudly. Instead of taking risks and showing off personality, it now feels like the franchise is trying to draw as little attention as possible, to avoid this continuous backlash.

It's a pity, because Star Wars is such a big sandbox, it makes no sense to focus only on what you want to see and disregard the rest. What has become clear in the ten years since Rogue One is that people don't actually want "gritty, realistic, and adult" stories, they want killing, one-liners and gratuitous demonstrations of strength for its own sake. They saw the form of Vader's hallway scene and loved it, while ignoring all the meaning and substance behind it.

Again, that scene belongs in Rogue One, and it fits with Vader's overall character, but, even through that lens, it's still misunderstood. People see that and think Vader is a badass, but he really isn't; he's just super strong and easily manipulated. Considering all we know about him from the Prequels and the Original Trilogy, he is actually quite pitiful. His hallway scene doesn't exist in a vacuum, so it shouldn't be viewed that way, either. Before we get more of that, we should really understand everything it really means.

Rogue One: A Star Wars Story is available to stream on Disney+.

As Chargers look for answers, who is to blame for 0-7 start?

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The Los Angeles Chargers' season has proven to be a complete failure.

The Chargers were predicted to be an above .500 team this season, but with a 0-7 start, the team now has very little wiggle room to even make that expectation a reality.

Coming off a bye week, the team did not appear to have taken any significant strides, suffering a 24-9 loss to the Los Angeles Rams at SoFi Stadium on Nov. 1.

The Chargers put together a promising first half, scoring nine points on three field goals.

One of the biggest positives from the half came in the final seconds of the second quarter when quarterback Justin Herbert threw a hail mary 54 yards to Ladd McConkey.

Unfortunately for the Chargers, McConkey was tackled at the one-yard line as time expired and went into the locker room without additional points.

The play didn't help create any momentum for the Chargers, who finished scoreless in the second half.

The Chargers will have the opportunity to turn things around against the Houston Texans on Sunday, Nov. 7, at home.

After seven consecutive losses this season, who is to blame for the disappointing start to the season?

There is a lot of blame that could be passed around regarding the team's recent on-field struggles.

Coach Jim Harbaugh, offensive coordinator Mike McDaniel and quarterback Justin Herbert are among the three in that order that should take on the bulk of that responsibility.

The Chargers moved away from being a pass-heavy offense that proved to be functional and efficient compared to what the offense has presented under McDaniel's watch this season.

Harbaugh added McDaniel to lead the offensive staff with the expectation that he would be an upgrade over what was already in place.

Herbert has been an above-average quarterback during his time in Los Angeles and he should be in the midst of a career year as a veteran and not regress with an uptick in interceptions.

As the head coach, Jim Harbaugh should take the brunt of the blame and responsibility.

He had the opportunity during the bye week to make much-needed changes to the offense but did nothing.

"We're aware of what the issues are, and we're the only ones that can fix them," Harbaugh told reporters on Nov. 1.

Harbaugh continued to get peppered with questions during the media availability but remained fairly vague.

"Our motivation is a dedication to getting it changed, getting it fixed," Harbaugh said when asked about keeping McDaniel.

While there was a pledge that some changes would be made, McDaniel remained as the playcaller through the bye week.

The Chargers have actually doubled down on their support and loyalty for one another.

Offensive lineman Joe Alt said the group remains bought in but admitted his displeasure with the results.

"Just frustrated," Alt said. "You want to come away with a win as a player and as a unit we have to be better. The scoreboard reflects that. … We have to score more points."

The offense has not scored a touchdown in its last nine quarters of play. It's a drought that spans 27 possessions.

Sean McVay vs Jim Harbaugh: Will Rams or Chargers figure things out?

As offensive coordinator with a clear directive from the head coach to score points, McDaniel's offense failed another test.

"We're not scoring enough points," Harbaugh said on Oct. 18 after a loss to the Kansas City Chiefs on the road.

McDaniel talked about collaborating with Herbert to help turn things around during the bye week. The coordinator called the bye week not much of a break, because "there were problems to solve."

The duo spent four hours watching film together throughout the week to "find spots to build on, where you can find spots to improve," but those again didn't necessarily result in touchdowns and a victory.

"In this league you have to produce results, or there's going to be heat, so I don't expect anything else," McDaniel said during a press conference on Oct. 29.

The heat is on, but it remains unclear when Harbaugh may be ready to make a change or if the organization will make it for him.

Last season, the Chargers had a middling offense under coordinator Greg Roman. It ranked 20th in the league with 21.6 points per game. The offense threw 14 interceptions and scored 36 total touchdowns.

This season, the Chargers rank last in scoring offense, averaging 14.1 points per game. The team is also averaging 310.9 total yards per game and has just nine total touchdowns through the first seven games.

Despite all the struggles Herbert has dealt with this season, he remains publicly bought into McDaniel's offense.

"I believe in him," Herbert said. "He's a great coach. He's very smart and it's on us as an offense to make those plays. ... We just have to make plays and score points and score touchdowns and we haven't done that for him."

Herbert completed 24 of 32 passes for 257 yards and two interceptions against the Rams on Sunday. He was also sacked twice.

"Whatever we've been doing hasn't been enough and it's up to us," Herbert said during the postgame press conference on Nov. 1. "Only we in the locker room can change it and work toward it."

Herbert has averaged 24 passing touchdowns and 9.7 interceptions per season through his career. This season has seen remarkable regression.

The star QB has completed 131 of his 212 pass attempts for 1,398 yards with just five touchdowns through the first seven games. He's already thrown 10 interceptions and has been sacked 23 times this season.

He's also second in rushing for the Chargers with 33 carries for 183 yards and three touchdowns.

"We don't really pay attention to the opinions of outside people," McDaniel told the media on Nov. 5. "People are saying whatever they want with whatever facts they have when we're not living up to expectations. ... Keep it moving."

This article originally appeared on USA TODAY: As Chargers look for answers, who is to blame for 0-7 start?

To Make a Doc About a Nomadic Cyclist, the ‘Perfect Road’ Filmmakers Spent 3 Months in a Campervan

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Adam and Christian Bonke recall how they and their cinematographer followed Maximilian Schnell in a "not so big" vehicle with "a lot of equipment" to shoot material for the film now screening at Tallinn.

For their documentary Perfect Road, Danish directors and cousins Adam and Christian Bonke followed nomadic cyclist Maximilian Schnell on an extreme journey across some of Europe’s highest mountain passes. The film documents his journey, offers a character study and serves up a meditation on modern life and our priorities and life choices.

Elk Film and Dogwoof produced Perfect Road, with Andreas Dalsgaard, Anna Godas, Oli Harbottle, Miriam Nørgaard serving as producers. Jonatan Mose served as cinematographer, with editing handled by Martin Anthon. Related Stories Movies PÖFF Stands With Ukraine: A Look at Films Featured at Tallinn Made Despite, and Because of, the War Oscars 2027: Int'l Films in Focus 'Hold Onto Me,' Cyprus' First Oscar Hopeful, Stars a Newcomer and a 'Dogtooth' Actor in a Very Personal Father-Daughter Drama

Perfect Road, which world premiered at Zurich, is now screening at the 30th anniversary edition of the Tallinn Black Nights Film Festival (PÖFF) in Estonia. It shows how the road brings freedom and adventure, but also grueling physical and mental challenges. And that is not only true for Schnell but also the directors and their cinematographer.

“We ended up living in a campervan for three months,” recalls Adam Bonke. “It was three grown-up men in a not so big campervan with a lot of equipment. And then we had a small a smaller car, like a satellite, that we could send out on the tiny roads where the campervan couldn’t go.”

Adds Christian Bonke: “It was such a headache because you had to put water into the car, you had to empty the toilet. So when we were done shooting for the day, we had to take care of all that.”

The filmmakers considered various strategies for capturing footage. Initially, they wanted to use several different camera people over the course of the three-month adventure. “But then our DP was kicked out by his girlfriend,” Christian Bonke tells THR. “So he said, ‘I’m going to do all three months. This is perfect for me.’ And Adam and I took turns.”

It may not have been a holiday or super-comfortable, but Adam Bonke tells THR: “It was comfortable enough.” And it allowed the filmmakers to get a better feel for Schnell’s life choices. “He said, ‘I’m trading comfort for freedom,'” the co-director explains. “I think that’s that’s such a nice thing to say and really frames the film perfectly.”

The Danish filmmaker duo actually still fondly looks back at the experience shooting Perfect Road. “We often talk about how we miss those days. It was so beautiful,” says Christian Bonke. “You felt embraced by nature. And after meeting Max, we were completely hit by how conventional and boring [traditional life can be] and how many constraints our lives had at home. So we could get a little glimpse of living his way.”

Netflix's Upcoming Sci-Fi Spinoff Takes Exciting Step Forward With New Update

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Netflix is preparing for the arrival of one of its highly anticipated spinoffs.

Netflix's upcoming Manifest spinoff series, Arrivals: A New Manifest Mystery, has reached another important milestone as development continues on the science fiction drama. After the original show concluded its run three years ago, the franchise is preparing to introduce an entirely new story within its universe. However, while the plot might be new, the spinoff will maintain its connections to the lore that made its predecessor successful.

Created by Jeff Rake, Manifest originally premiered on NBC in 2018 and followed the passengers of Montego Air Flight 828. Shortly after landing, the characters realized that more than five years had passed despite nothing seeming wrong with their flight. They begin experiencing mysterious events known as Callings, leading them to uncover disturbing secrets about their situation.

Although NBC canceled the series after three seasons, Netflix brought it back for a fourth and final season, which ended in June 2023. The show's popularity eventually led to another installment in the franchise, Arrivals, which Netflix officially announced in August 2026. Now, Rake has revealed that Arrivals: A New Manifest Mystery has secured a director for its first episode. The creator announced the news on his Instagram as part of his Showrunner Diaries series, which provides viewers with behind-the-scenes information.

While Rake confirmed that someone has been selected to direct the debut episode, the director's identity has not been disclosed at the time of this article's publication. The update also revealed that everything is progressing very smoothly across several departments. The creator explained that they are beginning to form Arrivals' production team, with several positions already filled.

Arrivals: A New Manifest Mystery was first announced by Netflix on August 28, 2026, with the date paying homage to Flight 828. The streaming platform confirmed that the upcoming project would feature new characters and storylines while staying true to the Manifest universe.

The spinoff series won't be a direct continuation of the original show's finale. Arrivals: A New Manifest Mystery will explore an entirely different incident within the franchise's world. The story reportedly begins when an unidentified aircraft makes an unexpected crash landing in Denver. The people aboard are unable to remember who they are or why they were on the plane in the first place.

There is no confirmed release date for Netflix's Arrivals: A New Manifest Mystery at the time of this article's publication. However, industry speculation predicts that the series will be released on the streaming platform in 2028.

A supernatural drama series created for NBC, Manifest is an investigation of a fictional flight, Montego Air Flight 828, that went missing for five years after a moment of turbulence in the sky. However, the passengers and crew only felt a moment pass despite the rest of the world assuming they were all gone. The "survivors" now try to reacclimate to society as the world has moved on without them - but voices from beyond seem to beckon them somewhere unknown.

Jason Bourne 6 Writer Confirms When Matt Damon Is Ready For Sequel With New Update

Matt Damon's return as Jason Bourne has received a promising new update, although the long-awaited sequel still faces one major condition.

Damon first played the former CIA assassin in 2002's The Bourne Identity, returning for The Bourne Supremacy (2004), The Bourne Ultimatum (2007), and Jason Bourne (2016). The action franchise also expanded with 2012's The Bourne Legacy, starring MCU Hawkeye actor Jeremy Renner as a different operative. Jon Bernthal's 100-Minute Taylor Sheridan Thriller Officially Being Removed From Netflix

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

When historians look back on the careers of various actors, 2026 will undoubtedly be one that stands out for Jon Bernthal. Best known for his role as The Punisher, Bernthal reprised this role in two separate MCU projects this year, The Punisher: One Last Kill and Spider-Man: Brand New Day. The latter grossed over $2 billion at the box office on the backs of Bernthal and Tom Holland’s star power, but it wasn’t the only film they starred in this year to find success at the box office.

Two weeks before the release of Spider-Man: Brand New Day, the duo starred in their first Christopher Nolan movie, The Odyssey. After The Odyssey grossed over $1.8 billion globally to become the highest-grossing R-rated feature ever, it is finally set to hit VOD in just a few weeks.

Just because Bernthal has had an impressive run of box office success, though, doesn’t mean all of his movies have had the same fate. Back in 2021, Bernthal starred in a Western thriller that grossed only $19 million at the box office, largely due to the restrictions that were still in place at many theaters due to the COVID-19 pandemic. We’re talking, of course, about Those Who Wish Me Dead, which was written and directed by Taylor Sheridan, who wrote all five seasons of Yellowstone.

Earlier this year, Those Who Wish Me Dead was added to Netflix, where it became one of the most-watched movies of the entire summer on the platform. However, all good things must come to an end, and the streamer recently confirmed that the last day to watch the movie on Netflix will be on November 30.

The show that claimed the most of your answers is the world you were built for. If two tied, both are shown — you're complicated enough to straddle two Sheridan universes.

You are a Dutton — or you might as well be. You understand that some things are worth protecting at any cost, and that the modern world's indifference to history, to land, to legacy, is not something you're willing to accept quietly. You lead from the front, you carry your family's weight without complaint, and when someone threatens what's yours, you don't escalate — you finish it. You're not cruel. But you are absolute. In Yellowstone's world, that combination of ferocity and loyalty doesn't make you a villain. It makes you the only thing standing between everything that matters and everyone who wants to take it.

You thrive in the chaos of high-stakes negotiation, where the money is enormous, the margins are thin, and the wrong word in the wrong room can cost everyone everything. You're a fixer — the person called when a situation is already on fire and needs someone with the nerve to walk into it. West Texas oil country rewards exactly what you are: sharp, adaptable, unsentimental, and absolutely clear-eyed about what people want and what they'll do to get it. You're not naive enough to think this world is fair. You're smart enough to be the one deciding who it's fair to.

You are a Dwight Manfredi — someone who has served their time, paid their dues, and arrived somewhere unexpected with nothing but their reputation and their wits. You adapt without losing yourself. You build loyalty through respect rather than fear, though you're not above reminding people that the two aren't mutually exclusive. Tulsa King is for people who are still standing when everyone assumed they'd be finished — who find, in an unfamiliar place, that they're more capable than the world gave them credit for. You don't need a throne. You build one, wherever you happen to land.

You carry the weight of a system that is broken by design, and you do it anyway — because someone has to, and because you're the only one positioned to do it without the whole thing collapsing. Mike McLusky's world is for people who are comfortable operating where there are no good options, only less catastrophic ones. You speak every language: law enforcement, criminal, political, human. That fluency makes you invaluable and it makes you a target. You've made your peace with both. Mayor of Kingstown belongs to people who understand that keeping the peace is not the same as being at peace — and who do the job regardless.

In addition to Jon Bernthal, Those Who Wish Me Dead also stars Academy Award winner Angelina Jolie and Superman’s Nicholas Hoult. Aidan Gillen, famed for his long-time role as Littlefinger in Game of Thrones, also stars in the film. The official synopsis for Those Who Wish Me Dead, which earned scores of 63% from critics and 86% from audiences on Rotten Tomatoes, reads as follows: “Oscar winner Jolie stars as Hannah, a smoke jumper still reeling from the loss of three lives she failed to save from a fire when she comes across a traumatized 12-year-old boy with nowhere else to turn.”

Check out Those Who Wish Me Dead before it’s removed from Netflix at the end of this month and stay tuned to Collider for more updates and coverage of Jon Bernthal’s future projects.

Stephen King's 13-Part Remake Of A Lovecraftian Classic Is A Shadow Of The Original

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Faith Roswell is a Senior Writer on Screen Rant's Classic TV team. Since earning her degree in Creative Writing over a decade ago, Faith has written articles on film and TV from a variety of different angles. Faith now combines her knowledge of psychology with her love of monster movies to give more insight into what makes the best ones.  You may have read her Screen Rant lists and features covering horror, sci-fi, and fantasy, or read her Amazon Top 10 book, "Movie Monsters of the Deep." Faith has had an extensive career as a writer, appearing on BBC live radio, researching true crime for Rotten Mango podcast, and writing for publications including Mental Floss, Atlas Obscura, and The Daily Jaws before beginning here at Screen Rant.

Stephen King is no stranger to Lovecraftian horrors, from IT's terrifying shapeshifter Pennywise to his mind-bending masterpiece The Dark Tower, but despite being known as "the king of horror," Stephen King's box office record is not always great. Many of his best adaptations have become the greatest horror movies of the past 50 years, but one of Stephen King's adaptations that missed the mark is, instead, best known for drawing attention to its fantastic source material. Lars von Trier is responsible for one of the 90s' most underrated must-watch horror shows, with King's version being a shadow of the original.

The Kingdom is a unique show that captures the surreal atmosphere of Twin Peaks, but with an even more disconcerting tone. Set within a hospital, Lars von Trier's 13-episode series follows its staff and patients as they encounter a bizarre range of supernatural phenomena. The show's three seasons are spaced years apart, first airing in 1994, and with the final season acting as the highly-anticipated ending to a cliffhanger viewers had been waiting 25 years to see resolved. The series is often considered to be one of von Trier's best works, with Stephen King's version failing to improve on it.

Stephen King's Kingdom Hospital was based on The Kingdom, but while the premise was still intriguing, King's version lacked the sheer weirdness that von Trier brought to the screen. The Kingdom had more than enough scenes that felt like nightmare fuel, but the dark satire included in the story was central to the show, while the adaptation adhered more closely to typical horror genre tropes. This meant that while Kingdom Hospital was a good show in its own right, earning two Emmy Award nominations, including for its visual effects, it did not bring anything new to TV.

The Kingdom was instantly recognizable for its sepia tone and for casting a wide range of character actors who could carry the story, which was often chaotic. Lars von Trier's characters tend to be realistically flawed people with trauma and obsessions, but this is a versatile enough description that his projects rarely feel repetitive. On the other hand, Stephen King's Kingdom Hospital included one of his most common self-inserts, the tormented writer. This changed the balance of the original series, drawing the focus to it being a Stephen King show, rather than the writer's take on the existing source material.

Lars von Trier's series is called Riget in Danish, meaning "the realm."

With Kingdom Hospital only adapting the first season of von Trier's original show, it failed to include the payoff that The Kingdom's fans had waited for, and instead felt like a confusing array of subplots compared with the original, which won 19 out of the 25 awards the series was nominated for. Had Kingdom Hospital been more streamlined and featured the uneasy Lovecraftian atmosphere that King incorporated into other projects, like The Mist, which had one of the most devastating endings of all time, the show could have come closer to matching the Danish original.

The Kingdom might not rank among the most Lovecraftian TV shows of all time, but it truly nails cosmic horror even so. Every aspect of the series, from its setting to its characters, hints at a vast unknown. From a pathologist obsessed with claiming a cancerous liver as a trophy, to a ghostly ambulance that appears and disappears, The Kingdom is extremely mysterious throughout, and rather than presenting the hauntings as disturbances that can be solved through killing a demonic power or solving an ancient curse, the series has no such neat ending.

Cosmic horror is designed to challenge our perception of reality, presenting unknown forces that are impossible for a human mind to comprehend, and The Kingdom's final act adds several more layers to the story, including a meta cameo from von Trier himself. Rather than being a direct Lovecraft copy or tribute, The Kingdom infuses elements of his work into the story, while remaining a recognizable von Trier project. Had Stephen King taken this approach, Kingdom Hospital would have been a very different, and potentially better, show.

Netflix Officially Adds Sylvester Stallone’s Explosive ’90s Survival Thriller

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

There was a time when Sylvester Stallone was seemingly incapable of making a movie that didn't involve putting himself through some sort of extraordinary physical punishment. Whether he was taking punches as Rocky Balboa or single-handedly dismantling entire armies as John Rambo, the action icon spent decades proving that there was almost nothing he couldn't survive. But in 1993, he decided to take things to an entirely different level, quite literally, by heading thousands of feet into the mountains for one of the most spectacular action thrillers of the decade.

Stallone's iconic action thriller Cliffhanger has officially arrived on Netflix in the United States, joining the platform on November 1, more than 33 years after its original theatrical release. Directed by Renny Harlin (Die Hard 2), the movie became an enormous box office success, earning $255 million worldwide against a reported $65 million production budget, and became one of Stallone's signature action films.

For Gabe Walker (Stallone) of Rocky Mountain Rescue, the mission sounds almost routine: locate five climbers lost among the icy peaks. But the distress call is really a trap set by merciless international terrorist Eric Qualen (John Lithgow). Now millions of dollars and the lives of Walker and the ones he loves hang in the balance. Against explosive firepower, bitter cold, and dizzying heights, Walker must outwit Qualen in a deadly game of hide-and-seek in this high-altitude avalanche of action and suspense.

The cast includes Sylvester Stallone (Rocky) as Gabe Walker, John Lithgow (Dexter) as Eric Qualen, Michael Rooker (Guardians of the Galaxy) as Hal Tucker, Janine Turner (Northern Exposure) as Jessie Deighan, Rex Linn (Better Call Saul) as Richard Travers, Caroline Goodall (Schindler's List) as Kristel, Leon (Cool Runnings) as Kynette, and Craig Fairbrass (Rise of the Footsoldier) as Delmar. It holds a 67% critics' score and 53% audience score on Rotten Tomatoes.

A reboot, featuring Lily James and Pierce Brosnan, is scheduled for release in 2027, directed by Jaume Collet-Serra. The remake was originally developed as a sequel to Stallone's original film, but after the star dropped out of the film's production, attached director Ric Roman Waugh left as well. In September 2025, a sequel was announced to be in development.

Cliffhanger is streaming now on Netflix in the United States.

‘Club Kid’ Scene-Stealer Kirby Howell-Baptiste on Playing a Drugged-Up Party Girl Who Turns Jordan Firstman’s World Upside Down: She’s ‘A Complete Tornado’

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

It’s a line said, in various forms, numerous times across the first half of “Club Kid,” Jordan Firstman‘s hedonistic and heartfelt coming-of-age comedy drama about a hard-partying New York club promoter suddenly forced to grapple with life and responsibilities. But the most hilariously-timed utterance comes in the middle of a crucial scene — one that shifts the entire movie — and from and by a minor, yet unforgettably wild, character. Related Stories Globo, Netflix Strike Biggest Licensing Deal Between the Two Companies, Led by Walter Salles’ Oscar Winner ‘I’m Still Here’ (EXCLUSIVE) Netflix’s 'Haunted Hotel' Renewed for Season 3

“She’s a massive party girl — she never sleeps, she only parties,” says Brit actor Kirby Howell-Baptiste of how Edison was described to her. “She’s completely lost all social boundaries. The lifetime of drugs melted half of her brain away, and all her social cues and etiquette have gone.”

Howell-Baptiste’s etiquette-free Edison is only in “Club Kid” for a few minutes, but manages to steal the crucial scene in question from under the nose of writer, director and lead star Firstman.

Roughly quarter of the way into the film, she rocks up unannounced and chaotic, sporting green braids and oversized sunglasses, at the apartment lived in by Firstman’s nightclub promoter Peter (Firstman), with 10-year-old Arlo (Reggie Absolom) in tow. While wandering around the place like it’s her own, she reveals — in a hysterically matter-of-fact manner — that not only is Arlo his, but she’s brought the kid across from the U.K. to live with him permanently. Boom.

But Peter is gay — and confidently declares that he’s never slept with a woman. Not true, Edison asserts as she casually roams the kitchen on the hunt for literally anything remotely looking like drugs she can swallow or snort. He had sex with her friend Leonine a decade earlier at one of his nights while so high on ketamine, cocaine, GHB and (probably) more that he didn’t have a clue what was going on.

“Nah, you fucked her, babes,” Edison says. “Point is, that’s your son.”

Tragically, Leonine — as her apparent friend explains while sniffing some white powder she found on the counter — struggled with mental health issues (“She was in and out of the loony bin… what do you call it, rehab?”) and recently ended her life. “Last week she goes and tops herself — she fucking killed herself, babe.”

In the midst of making these life-changing and upsetting revelations in the most wonderfully insensitive manner possible, Edison inquires if Peter and her should “do a bump” of coke (slightly shaken by how his life is quickly unravelling, he declines).

“[Edison] is just a complete tornado,” says Londoner Howell-Baptiste, adding that she thinks the character is an “amalgamation of a few party people” Firstman has encountered. “But what I really love is that, although she’s absolutely bananas, she does feel quite real and quite relatable. I think everyone knows that person, to some degree. At some point in your life, you have met that person.”

Edison isn’t seen again in “Club Kid” after the apartment scene, in which she eventually leaves Arlo in Peter’s care and — predictably — fails to return to collect him as promised later on. But as Peter begins a frantic search to find her across various New York house parties, further levels of her comically debauched behaviour come to light, as he hears how she was kicked out of several places for simply being too much of a tornado to handle.

“I wasn’t part of those scenes, obviously, but what was really cool was that you got more of a picture of Edison,” she says. “Even in her absence, you got more of a picture of what she was doing and how she was just belligerently running around New York, hoovering up drugs at everyone’s party.”

Howell-Baptiste admits she had no idea who Firstman was when she was originally invited to audition for “Club Kid” in mid 2025. As someone who’s “not really on social media much” she missed his breakout during the COVID-19 pandemic. But she checked out some of his Instagram videos and thought he was funny. Crucially, the casting was being done by Carmen Cuba, who she says has “really exquisite taste” and thought, while she hadn’t seen the full script, the role seemed fun — “and the character was a party girl and I felt like I could channel that pretty well.”

A few months later, shooting in New York, Howell-Baptiste still didn’t have a full picture of what “Club Kid” was all about (she admits to only watching it in September), but her main takeaway was that Edison was written by someone “who knows how to party.”

While the scene they shot was “almost verbatim” from the audition, there was a bit of ad-libbing, with Howell-Baptiste being the one who added the British slang term “tops herself.”

“And afterwards, everyone was like, ‘What do you you mean ‘”tops herself”?’ Because, obviously, we’re in a film that’s written by a gay man. There’s so much about it that celebrates queer culture, and topping has a completely different meaning there,” she says. Despite offering to remove the word, she was told to keep it in. “It made the cut — and it think it’s really fun and adds even more confusion when it comes to American audiences being like: ‘What is going on with this girl?'”

In terms of notes from Firstman, she says the main discussion was how he wanted Edison’s voice to sound, with the request that — alongside the British accent — she was “very hoarse, like she’s been up all night and almost always lost her voice.”

To achieve this, Howell-Baptiste channelled former Spice Girl Mel B. “She always had this slight hoarseness singing that I always thought was very cool, so that’s where my mind went,” she says.

Having shot her key scene (and the even shorter scene in which Edison and Leonine first meet Peter in the club) in the fall of 2025, Howell-Baptiste — whose long line of credits include “Killing Eve,” “Barry” and “Hacks” — moved on to other acting jobs. Several months later, while filming Season 3 of British TV drama “Sherwood,” Firstman messaged her to tell her that “Club Kid” had been accepted into Cannes. “I was like, ‘How incredible,’ but that was sort of it for me for a very long time.”

Of course, “Club Kid” wound up being the talk of the festival festival, the breakout hit that sparked an intense bidding war eventually won by A24 for the princely sum of $17 million. Howell-Baptiste was oblivious to it all (“I joke that I live under a rock”) until her manager messaged her saying “Are you aware of everything that’s going on with ‘Club Kid’ in Cannes?” Keeping with the theme, it was a makeup artist who later informed her that “Club Kid” was going to Toronto. “I just wasn’t aware of any of the buzz!”

Thankfully, Howell-Baptiste was able to attend the premiere at the BFI London Film Festival and took to the stage at the Royal Festival Hall with Firstman and Absolom, staying to watch the film to experience the laughs, cheers and tears from a hugely enthusiastic audience. Now, she can finally enjoy having a crucial — and spectacularly funny — role in one of the standout movies of the year.

“I filmed in a complete vacuum — I just did one scene with three other characters and filmed the party scene, which was just one night,” she says. “So it’s all come as a really pleasant surprise to me — it’s lovely.”

The 6 Best Thriller Shows Released Since 2010

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Sir Gary Oldman in "Slow Horses," now streaming on Apple TV.

Based on the types of television shows still being produced, the thriller remains near the top. Encompassing a large spectrum of subgenres, the thriller provides audiences the chance to get their heart pumping from the safety of their own home. With network television and streaming platforms taking bigger and bolder risks, the thriller has been getting better and better. Since 2010, we’ve seen a rise in high-quality thrillers, and these six titles are proof.

From a masterpiece anthology series based on a ‘90s classic to a crime thriller lifted from an iconic novel series, these six shows got adrenaline rushing as people kept talking. While a plethora of other incredible thrillers have come out over the last decade and a half, they don’t hold a candle to these.

Who says comedies can’t be thrilling? In the mystery comedy hit Only Murders in the Building, three eccentric neighbors, Charles-Haden Savage (Steve Martin), Oliver Putnam (Martin Short), and Mabel Mora (Selena Gomez), live in their upscale New York City apartment building, the Arconia, and share an intense obsession with true crime podcasts. When a suspicious, gruesome death occurs inside their building, the trio teams up to investigate the truth using their knowledge of the genre, recording their own podcast to document the case. As they untangle a web of secrets and mounting clues, they quickly realize a ruthless killer might be living right among them. A cozy murder mystery series created by Martin and John Hoffman, Only Murders in the Building redefined the modern murder-mystery genre.

Instead of a grim, linear police procedural, the series pokes fun at and analyzes society's obsession with true-crime podcasts while still delivering layered, twist-filled whodunits. A genuine masterclass in performance, the legendary comedy duo elevate their costar to create a unique, multi-generational dynamic that grounds high-stakes apartment-building murders in genuine warmth and character-driven humor. The show has become a destination for some of Hollywood’s biggest names to join in the fun, including Meryl Streep, Paul Rudd, Tina Fey, Tea Leoni, Renée Zellweger, and many more. For the next season, they’ll be welcoming some of Britain’s best, including Olivia Colman, David Tennant, Nicola Coughlan, Jodie Whittaker, and Martin Freeman, among others. A brilliant escapist thriller, Only Murders in the Building is entertainment at its finest.

The Breaking Bad blueprint has been attempted to be replicated often since its final season. Very few shows found success. Ozark was one. Created by Bill Dubuque and Mark Williams, the four-season series follows Marty Byrde (Jason Bateman), a financial planner who is forced to move his family from Chicago to the Missouri Ozarks after a money-laundering scheme for a Mexican drug cartel goes dangerously wrong. To keep his family alive, Marty must wash $500 million in five years, dragging his ambitious wife Wendy and their children deeper into a dark world of local criminals, corporate corruption, and ruthless drug lords. As the family adapts to their new criminal reality, they evolve from desperate survivors into a formidable and calculating political powerhouse. Ozark became one of the defining crime thrillers of the 21st century due to its relentless tension, strong acting, and unique atmosphere.

Like Breaking Bad, Ozark strips away filler episodes and continually ramps up the stakes for Marty and Wendy Byrde (Laura Linney), as they navigate Mexican drug cartels, the FBI, and local criminals. Ozark uses the grim, blue-tinted, and menacing lakefront setting of the Missouri Ozarks to amplify its suffocating dread. The writing regularly defies traditional crime-genre tropes, refusing to give characters easy moral redemptions or predictable fates. The series boasted some sensational performances, perhaps none better than Julia Garner as Ruth Langmore. The range and depth she gave the morally troubled young woman became the heartbeat of the series. While some fans felt the series stumbled toward the end, Ozark remains one of Netflix’s greatest products. ​​​​​​​

Four criminal masterminds. One of them was always going to be yours. Here is who comes for you, why they picked you out of everyone available, and what they want from the encounter.

Your nemesis does not want to beat you. He wants to know you completely, and he is patient enough to wait for it. The frightening part is how good the conversation is.

Your nemesis is not after money, territory or revenge. He is after a demonstration, and you are the person he has chosen to prove his point on.

Your nemesis respects you, which sounds flattering until you work out what it means. He chose you because nobody else was worth his time.

Your nemesis has no personal interest in you whatsoever, and that is precisely what makes him unsurvivable. You are not a rival. You are a stop on the way.

Perhaps the very best spy thriller ever made, The Americans subverted traditional spy tropes. Created by Joe Weisberg, the series The Americans follows Philip and Elizabeth Jennings (Matthew Rhys and Keri Russell), two deep-cover Soviet KGB officers posing as a typical American married couple in the suburbs of Washington, D.C. While raising their unsuspecting children and managing the psychological complexities of their arranged relationship, they carry out high-stakes espionage right under the nose of their neighbor, an FBI counterintelligence agent. The Americans anchors high-stakes international espionage in the grueling, realistic intimacy of marriage, creating an agonizing, slow-burning tension that is deeply psychological. Running for six sensational seasons, The Americans features one of the most meticulously written, flawless final seasons and series finales in television history.

At its core, the show uses the extreme parameters of being KGB sleeper agents—lying, identity theft, seduction, and assassination—as a mirror for the complexities of a long-term relationship. Starting with an arranged marriage, it slowly morphs into genuine affection and love. The real suspense comes not just from whether they get caught by their FBI-agent neighbor, Stan Beeman (Noah Emmerich), but from whether their relationship can survive shifting ideological weight, mutual betrayals, and the profound psychological toll of their double lives. The Americans treats violence with grounding, bleak realism. Actions have permanent consequences; disposing of a body is a messy, bone-breaking chore, and close-quarters combat is exhausting and brutal. The show never lets the audience forget the immense physical and emotional cost of the characters' choices. The series pulls off the narrative magic trick of making Western audiences deeply empathize with Soviet operatives whose literal directive is to destabilize the United States. It’s all thanks to the sensational performances at top billing.​​​​​​​

One of the greatest films of the ‘90s is Fargo. Two decades later, it served as the basis for a brilliant anthology series, using the same DNA. Created by Noah Hawley, the five-season series set in the frozen Midwest features a different self-contained story that follows a different cast of ordinary citizens, ruthless criminals, and law enforcement officers as seemingly minor choices spiral into chaotic violence. With its unique blend of pulse-pounding suspense, dark humor, and philosophical depth, Fargo became a thematic masterpiece where the niceties of the North were the perfect juxtaposition for an examination of the nature of evil, systemic greed, luck, and the slippery definition of "a true story".

Fargo masterfully walks a tightrope between grim, white-knuckle neo-noir violence and absurd Midwestern dark comedy. The mundane politeness of small-town life sharply contrasts with chaotic, high-stakes criminality. Rather than serving up cheap thrills, Hawley uses the Coen brothers' tonal legacy to dig into the core themes: the chaotic intersection of ordinary life and brutal crime. The show consistently contrasts absolute, often personified malice against ordinary, kind-hearted Midwestern law enforcement officers trying to maintain a baseline of human decency. Morality tales are deeply steeped in black comedy and cosmic irony, highlighting how random coincidence or sheer human stupidity can dictate life-or-death consequences. By resetting the cast, setting, and decade each season, ranging from 1950s Kansas City to 2019 Minnesota, the show avoids the narrative fatigue that traps long-running serialized crime dramas. That means a top-tier revolving door of stars had fun in the freezing, including Billy Bob Thornton, Martin Freeman, Kirsten Dunst, Jesse Plemons, Carrie Coon, Ewan McGregor, Chris Rock, Jessie Buckley, Juno Temple, and Jon Hamm, among others. The show had five fabulous seasons, but die-hard fans would die for more! ​​​​​​​

Though it is the antithesis of polished spycraft like James Bond, Slow Horses is an espionage thriller at its finest. Based on Mick Herron’s novel series, the Will Smith-created series is a darkly comedic thriller that follows a group of disgraced British intelligence agents banished to a dumping-ground department called Slough House after career-ending mistakes. Under the leadership of their brilliant but notoriously abrasive and unkempt boss, Jackson Lamb (Gary Oldman), they are expected to endure dull paperwork but repeatedly find themselves entangled in dangerous conspiracies that threaten national security. Despite their "loser" status, the team must use their flawed skills to outmaneuver the mainstream MI5 operatives and save the day. Slow Horses subverts the exhausting, hyper-slick tropes of modern espionage in favor of working-class competency, pitch-black humor, and flawless pacing. Slow Horses has perfected a formula that keeps it operating at the absolute peak of the television landscape.

Unlike the untouchable spy thrillers of the era, Slow Horses platforms exiled duds and burnouts. Yet, the show avoids making them bumbling caricatures. They are deeply broken people dealing with real-world issues like addiction, divorce, and professional trauma, but when backed into a corner, their underlying spy training kicks in. Led by Oldman, Slow Horses is a tried-and-true ensemble series where each character has their own distinct identity. You love them for their flaws. Slow Horses masterfully balances pulse-pounding, high-stakes danger with laugh-out-loud workplace dark comedy. It treats the violence, institutional rot, and geopolitical threats with severe gravity, but lets the mundane reality of administrative purgatory provide a grounded, human contrast. Slow Horses operates with old-school efficiency. Perhaps the most genius decision the series has made is keeping each season at a tightly plotted six episodes. There are zero filler episodes, zero unnecessary flashbacks, and no narrative treading water. Each season, with one exception, adapts a single, self-contained book from Herron’s series, establishing a clear beginning, middle, and explosive end.

If there’s one crime thriller we wished wasn’t a one-and-done limited series wonder, it’s Mare of Easttown. Created by Brad Ingelsby, the gritty drama follows world-weary detective Mare Sheehan (Kate Winslet) as she investigates the brutal murder of a local teenage mother in a small, close-knit Pennsylvania town. As the investigation uncovers dark, long-hidden community secrets, she simultaneously struggles to keep her own personal life from shattering under the weight of divorce, custody battles, and the unresolved trauma of her son's suicide. A powerful story that moves beyond typical police procedural, Mare of Easttown subverts the traditional "whodunit" tropes by prioritizing devastating character realism over sensationalized twists. The series functions as a richly authentic study of grief, generational trauma, and community decay.

Unlike slick, stylized Hollywood thrillers, Mare of Easttown grounds its mystery in the harsh realities of the modern American Rust Belt, dealing heavily with the fallout of the opioid epidemic, poverty, and systemic despair. The series drops audiences into the lived-in DelCo part of Pennsylvania, ripe with pitch-perfect accents and localized texture. The show treats its setting with immense respect and gritty accuracy. It captures the claustrophobia of a small town where everyone is interconnected, everyone shares the same history, and every suspect is someone's cousin, childhood friend, or ex-lover. Winslet is at her finest, shedding her movie-star aesthetic to play a flawed, vape-smoking, unglamorous, exhausted, grieving mother and grandmother who happens to be a detective. Joined by a top-tier ensemble, every character feels like real people you'd meet in a struggling Pennsylvania town. A smartly plotted and tightly presented masterpiece, Mare of Easttown is a devastating, emotionally logical thriller.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

ABC Delivers Devastating News For The Rookie's Most Toxic Couple

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Cher Thompson is a Senior Staff Writer at Screen Rant, where she covers everything from explosive reality TV moments to cast interviews about the twists and turns of scripted dramas. With a background in creative writing, marketing, and teaching, Cher knows how to spot both the storytelling genius and the guilty pleasures in pop culture. Her work blends wit with insight, making readers feel like they’re chatting with a friend who just happens to know way too much about TV.

With The Rookie season 9 coming during ABC's 2026-2027 mid-season, a disappointing update about one of the show's most established couples makes it even more clear why the pair is problematic. Throughout the run of The Rookie, viewers have had the chance to weigh in on their favorite romances, although the show has never entirely been about the Mid-Wilshire team's personal relationships. As John Nolan (Nathan Fillion) has worked his way up the ranks within the LAPD, both him and those surrounding him have had several different relationships, keeping romance alive and well as The Rookie has continued to flourish.

Throughout the last few seasons, the majority of relationships on the show have solidified. From Nolan himself finally marrying his longtime love Bailey Nune (Jenna Dewan) during The Rookie's 100th episode to Angela Lopez (Alyssa Diaz) and Wesley Evers (Shawn Ashmore) welcoming their second child, the show's established couples have been through major changes. Meanwhile, others like Tim Bradford (Eric Winter) and Lucy Chen (Melissa O'Neil) have been finding their footing, with The Rookie season 8 finale finally seeing the long-term slow burn get engaged, ending their seasons long will they/won't they dynamic. The show's most difficult couple, however, has fluctuated over the past few seasons.

Despite having much less to do than in past seasons, Nyla Harper (Mekia Cox) and her husband, James Murray (Arjay Smith) remained one of the most difficult pairs to watch, and The Rookie season 9 seems like it won't offer much to change their tough dynamic moving forward.

In a recent chat with Cox, Matt Mitovitch confirmed on Matt's Inside Line that James and Nyla's marriage is holding strong during The Rookie season 9. While Cox revealed the disappointing news that the couple won't be separated in the coming season, she did tease that things may not be easy between the pair when the series comes back to ABC. Sharing the couple will be facing an "obstacle" that they're working to overcome as a couple, Cox did reveal that whatever's coming for the pair "causes Nyla to make some 'interesting' decisions." While that may mean things are getting complicated for Nyla, it keeps her marriage to James firmly in view.

Throughout their time together on The Rookie, James and Nyla's relationship has always been a struggle. First meeting during The Rookie season 3, James' values didn't align with Nyla's from the very beginning of their relationship, and though they have similar overall goals with their work, their paths to get there are completely incompatible. James, who works as an advocate for the community and is often against Nyla's work specifically, has had tough moments in the rocky relationship, while Nyla has often struggled to figure out how to keep the pair's blended family together despite the drama surrounding them consistently.

Despite the fact that the pair have been married since The Rookie season 4, James and Nyla's relationship has been rocky at best for the duration of their time together on the show. With their overall ideals consistently struggling to mesh, the pair haven't been able to see eye to eye over the course of the last few seasons. While their relationship with Angela and Wesley seems to keep them balanced to a degree, James and Nyla's confrontations over her work being the antithesis to everything he believes are consistent and unending, without much progress made when they do discuss things.

Though the tension between the couple often keeps them fighting for their relationship, it also makes it clear that there are fundamental differences in their marriage that they simply haven't found a way to work through. With both James and Nyla having struggled with focusing on each other in the past, the pair's conflicts never truly seem to be resolved, despite how often they try to work through their problems. With Nyla being one of The Rookie's strongest characters, it's difficult to see her in a relationship where she doesn't always feel respected in the ways she should be.

Star Wars Jedi: Fallen Order Officially Returns Next Month

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Star Wars Jedi: Fallen Order is easily one of the best Star Wars ever made. Luckily, Star Wars Jedi: Survivor is the perfect sequel, in that it didn't drop the ball and effortlessly improves on the original in every possible way. With a third and final entry officially confirmed, EA and Respawn Entertainment have the chance to deliver the first truly perfect Star Wars trilogy. Really think about it, there has never been a perfect Star Wars trilogy. Return of the Jedi is, at best, pretty good.

Anyway, while we wait for an official update on the next Star Wars Jedi game, we'll be reuniting with Cal Kestis in December 2026, when the third volume of the ongoing Star Wars Jedi manga will release. For those who aren't up to speed with this series, the Star Wars Jedi: Fallen Order manga offers up an action-packed retelling of the 2019 game of the same name.

Volume 2 of the manga, which released in May this year, follows the part of the game in which Cal explored Zeffo and Dathomir and takes readers up to the moment his lightsaber is destroyed and he's left at his lowest point. It also shows us his first encounter and duel with the Second Sister, which is just as stunning as it was in the game. Now, we have official confirmation that the story will continue in December.

“The multi-volume manga adaptation of the best-selling Star Wars video game continues," reads a synopsis from online retailer Blackwells. "The saga of Cal Kestis continues in volume three of the manga adaptation of the hit video game! Haunted by tragic memories, and with his lightsaber destroyed, Cal’s quest is about to test him in ways he never could’ve imagined. As he journeys to new planets, Cal delves into his painful past and his relationship with his deceased master. With striking visuals and an action-packed story, readers will be immersed in Cal’s quest like never before.”

Some of the wilder rumors suggested Star Wars Jedi 3 could be announced this year and see a 2026 release, though given we're now well into November 2026, I'd be astonished if that actually happened. If I had to guess, I'd say perhaps we'll see an announcement for the third game at The Game Awards in December followed by an early(ish) 2027 release, but I have to stress that really is just a guess. I know nothing. About anything. Ever.

Hopefully the third and (I assume) final volume of the manga will scratch your Star Wars Jedi itch until the third game lands. In other Star Wars news, it finally looks like we're getting Battlefront 3, although in the ultimate monkey's paw situation, it appears the new Star Wars FPS has come at the expense of Mass Effect 5, which was scrapped by EA alongside beloved RPG studio BioWare. Were we robbed? Yes, obviously. Here's hoping one day we live in a world where Mass Effect and Star Wars are both thriving.
