Game Of Thrones Star Who Played Jon Snow's Key Ally Has 1 Condition For Potential Spinoff Return

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

At least one major Game of Thrones character would be expected to return for a Jon Snow spinoff.

Earlier this year, star Kit Harington confirmed that the Jon Snow spinoff isn't officially dead, despite previous reports that it had been cancelled. The sequel has been tentatively in development since as early as 2022, the year Game of Thrones' first spinoff series, House of the Dragon, premiered. This was three years after Game of Thrones' ending, the last time we saw several characters closely associated with Jon Snow.

Now, in a new interview with Radio Times, actor John Bradley revealed how he could imagine himself reprising his iconic role as Jon's best friend, Samwell Tarly. "I think it all depends on the script and what the treatment of that character would be," he said. "When I think about Samwell, I just feel I can't do it anymore. Like I don't know what the voice is, and I don't know what the mannerisms are."

Bradley had been a member of the cast since season 1, when Sam arrived at Castle Black in the fourth episode. Jon quickly came to like and respect Sam, as Sam didn't attempt to lie about his own shortcomings or why his father threatened him into joining the Night's Watch. As of the series' finale, Sam was in King's Landing serving as King Bran Stark's (Isaac Hempstead Wright) Grand Maester.

Bradley went on to say: "So it'd be of no interest if I was to go back and play that character in the place that we left him. But if you could revisit that character and have a new experience of them and see that he's changed and see the way how he adapts to different situations, then of course because you're extending the biography of the character that you know."

This sequel series has the potential to pick up years later. In the finale, Jon rejoins the Night Watch, then goes out beyond the Wall with Tormund Giantsbane (Kristofer Hivju). He presumably ends up in some position of leadership among the Free Folk and the Night's Watch and would be involved if a new threat emerged. For Sam to be included, he would have to be in a different place in his life, if it meant leaving his job in King's Landing.

"But it has to be really worth playing and really add something to the history of that character that was totally new and totally unexpected for me to have an interest in it," Bradley concluded. Additionally, Sam's personal life will affect who he is in this spinoff; at the end of Game of Thrones, he and Gilly (Hannah Murray) are expecting their second child.

Meanwhile, the Game of Thrones fandom is awaiting the fourth and final season of House of the Dragon, which will probably arrive in the summer of 2028. Following its sensational premiere this year, A Knight of the Seven Kingdoms will return for season 2 in 2027, while the Aegon's Conquest movie is confirmed and slated for 2029.

However, rumored sequels focusing on fan-favorite characters from the flagship remain so fascinating because of how difficult they would be to pull off, given Game of Thrones' controversial ending. Sam's return may boost the chances of the Jon Snow spinoff succeeding, though it could be trickier to incorporate him organically than a character like Tormund, who is in the same place as Jon in the finale.

All episodes of Game of Thrones are available to stream on HBO Max.

Jamie Lee Curtis Details Close Bond With Rob Reiner's Kids Jake, Romy

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Jamie Lee Curtis shared how she’s supporting Rob Reiner and Michele Singer Reiner’s kids Jake Reiner and Romy Reiner nearly a year after their parents’ deaths: “We are family.”

Jamie Lee Curtis is holding Jake Reiner and Romy Reiner close as they continue to navigate their family tragedy.

Nearly one year after Rob Reiner and his wife Michele Singer Reiner were murdered in their Los Angeles home, The Bear alum—a longtime friend of the family—shed light on her bond with the late couple’s kids as they continue to grieve the loss of their parents.

“Of course, I mean, we are family,” Jamie shared in an interview with Extra published Nov. 6 of her bond with Romy, 28, and Jake, 35. “Something like this brings people so much closer.”

“Jake and I were sitting shoulder-to-shoulder doing an interview,” she continued, “and just feeling him there is emotional, and then of course him talking about his parents and their fantastic relationship.”

It’s been over 10 months since Rob and Michele were found dead in their Brentwood, Calif., home on Dec. 14.

In July, the couple’s 32-year-old son Nick Reiner was indicted by a grand jury for their murders, with the grand jury alleging that he intentionally killed his parents by means of lying in wait with “a deadly or dangerous weapon”—in this case a knife.

He has pleaded not guilty on two counts of murder with the special circumstance allegations of multiple murders and murder by means of lying in wait, according to Los Angeles County District Attorney Nathan J. Hochman.

And while Rob—who also shared daughter Tracy Reiner, 62, with ex Penny Marshall—and Michele were cremated a week after their tragic deaths, their loved ones have yet to gather in a public setting as they continue to mourn.

“There hasn’t been a big public memorial yet,” Jamie told Extra, “and so we’re all grieving.”

Yet, the Freaky Friday actress has made sure to honor Rob’s legacy on the world’s stage.

In fact, Jamie paid tribute to the late “cherished legend” during the 2026 Emmys In Memoriam segment in September, getting emotional as she highlighted Rob’s achievements, including winning a posthumous Emmy for his work on The Bear.

“He now holds the record of 48 years for the longest gap between acting Emmys, which was previously held by his father Carl Reiner at 37 years,” the Halloween alum shared. "He was generous and kind, and funny as hell, and a friend to all. And we all miss Rob and Michele.”

While looking back at her heartfelt tribute, Jamie—mom to kids Annie and Ruby with husband Christopher Guest—shared insight into her decades-long bond with the family.

“I’m obviously a close friend of theirs; we got married at Rob’s house on Dec. 18, 1984. Rob was the best man,” she recalled to The Hollywood Reporter in an interview published Oct. 23. “My late sister Kelly was my maid of honor. So we are close. Rob is our daughter Annie’s godfather. When Michele married Rob, she and Rob became my daughter’s godparents. We have a closeness, of course.”

As Jamie put it, “What I’m trying to say is that I felt a responsibility to represent everyone—strangers, fans, colleagues, friends, close friends and family—in that moment at the Emmys.”

To see how more stars have paid tribute to the Reiners, keep reading…

The actress, who Rob Reiner adopted during his marriage to late ex Penny Marshall, said she saw her dad at a family gathering the day before he and his wife Michele Singer Reiner were found dead in an apparent homicide at their Los Angeles home.

“I came from the greatest family ever,” she told NBC News in a Dec. 14 statement. “I don’t know what to say. I’m in shock.”

Thank you for your faith in the best in people, and for your profound love of our country."Meg continued of her When Harry Met Sally director and Michele, "I have to believe that their story will not end with this impossible tragedy, that some good may come, some awareness raised… I don’t know, but my guess is that they would want that to be hopeful and humane, to be something that brings us all to a greater understanding of one another and to some peace."

“Rob was a friend and collaborator through much of my life," the Spinal Tap actor shared to Instagram. "He was funny, he was smart, he was a mensch. When he came to see the comedy act I was in, and, later, the musical comedy I had co-written, his laugh was uproarious and audible around the block."

Rumour: Data Found In Zelda: Ocarina Of Time May Hint At Master Quest DLC

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

One of our main bugbears with Zelda: Ocarina of Time on Switch 2 – as incredible as it is – is that it's overall quite a breezy experience for those already familiar with the original or 3DS release.

As such, we thought it might beneficial if Nintendo were to include a more difficult mode, whether it be a simple Hero Mode in which enemies deal more damage, or perhaps even a port of the reworked Master Quest mode from the GameCube and 3DS.

Our prayers may (emphasis on the 'may') soon be answered, as a post from MrCheeze on BlueSky reveals that data found in Ocarina of Time may be hinting at some sort of Master Quest content. You'll see in the image below that many of the lines of data include 'MQ' next to the title 'DodongoCavern'.

MrCheeze has also provided more information in a follow-up post, stating "Couple strings in the exefs as well giving some context (HasMQ, IsMQModeExist, NewGameMQ, ReleaseMQ)".

So does this mean Master Quest is on the way? Potentially. We're not quite ready to put money on it, but it would certainly make sense for Nintendo to include it as part of a DLC expansion, whether free or paid.

Almost all first-party Nintendo games these days include some sort of post-launch DLC, so we've no reason to believe Ocarina of Time would be any different. As ever, though, do take all of this with a pinch of salt.

In a recent interview with IGN, producer Eiji Aonuma stated " I don't think that we would announce anything specific at this time" when asked whether Master Quest was considered for the Switch 2 remake.

The Lincoln Lawyer Season 5 Successfully Replaces Harry Bosch

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

While The Lincoln Lawyer season 5 won’t feature the title character of Prime Video’s Bosch after all, this could actually be good for the final chapter of Netflix’s legal drama. When Prime Video’s Bosch debuted in February 2014, it is fair to say that even committed fans of detective dramas could not have predicted how big an impact the series would have on the genre in the years that followed.

Adapted from Michael Connelly’s novel series of the same name, Bosch followed Titus Welliver’s titular LAPD homicide detective as he investigated murders while also keeping a close eye on his department’s own corruption issues. While HBO’s True Detective season 1, which arrived within a month of Bosch’s debut, is often called the most influential detective drama of the 2010s, Bosch’s franchise gives the critical hit a run for its money.

Without True Detective, viewers might never have gotten Sharp Objects, Mare of Easttown, and a slew of other acclaimed dark psychological thrillers. However, after Bosch’s seven-season run ended, the Connelly adaptation spawned the three-season sequel series Bosch: Legacy, the two-season spinoff Ballard, and the upcoming prequel Bosch: Start of Watch. The popularity of Connelly’s series even resulted in Netflix turning one of the author’s other novel series into a show with The Lincoln Lawyer.

Staring Manuel Garcia-Rulfo, The Lincoln Lawyer marks the second time Connelly’s troubled antihero, Mickey Haller, made it to the screen. Previously played by Matthew McConaughey in 2011’s standalone movie The Lincoln Lawyer, Haller is a conflicted former addict who gets his nickname thanks to his unconventional decision to run his legal practice out of the boot of his flashy Lincoln town car. Throughout The Lincoln Lawyer’s four seasons to date, Haller has slowly gotten his life back together, while always showing up in the courtroom.

Like Bosch, Haller can pull himself out of his personal troubles when his job calls for it. Netflix’s upcoming The Lincoln Lawyer season 5 was set to make the comparisons between the pair even more obvious, as the outing is based on Connelly’s 2025 novel Resurrection Walk. In the shared fictional universe of Connelly’s novels, Bosch and Haller are half-brothers, and Bosch spends much of Resurrection Walk assisting Haller in his latest case.

Sadly, the Bosch franchise belongs to Prime Video, while Netflix owns the rights to The Lincoln Lawyer. As such, the odds of an in-universe crossover were always vanishingly unlikely, and made even more implausible when Welliver moved on from the role. Although he played Bosch in a guest star capacity during 2025’s Ballard season 1, Welliver’s busy schedule means he won’t even be providing a voice-over for Bosch: Start of Watch.

As such, The Lincoln Lawyer season 5 ended up replacing Bosch’s role in Resurrection Walk with Cobie Smulders’s Emi, Mickey’s half-sister who was introduced in season 4. Emi is set to receive a spinoff of her own after The Lincoln Lawyer ends with season 5, meaning this is the perfect time to get viewers invested in her story.

Speaking to the Spokesman Review as part of the Northwest Passages conversation series, Connelly explained that Bosch became Mickey’s half-sister Emi in season 5’s reworked version of the novel’s plot, and said of the change that “it worked out pretty well.” Considering how much TV adaptations of beloved detectives like Karin Slaughter’s Will Trent often drastically alter their literary source material, it makes sense that Connelly isn’t worried about the change.

It would always have been near-impossible for Bosch and The Lincoln Lawyer to cross over, and not just because of the legal, practical questions of competing streaming services owning the rights to the characters. Bosch’s dark, grim neo-noir tone would clash massively with the melodramatic, sunny fun of Netflix’s The Lincoln Lawyer, a soapier, self-consciously sillier series with none of the former’s patient pacing.

Chauffeur Life

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

My guests’ expertise in rear-seat amenities provides some insight into passenger-pleasing design.

The kids sure like to drink. Just not alcohol. Data shows that’s down by a considerable percentage among young people. Good for them. No, the youths in my social circle like to stay hydrated. They are never without a water bottle (bedazzled or stickered to set it apart from everyone else’s Owalas and Stanleys) or a sweet tea with a fat straw from a boba shop.

On a recent weekend visit from my college bestie and her teen and tween daughters, we must have hit every bubble-beverage and smoothie spot in town. All of that chauffeuring made me hyperaware of what makes for a well-appointed back seat. Number one, according to my expert passengers? Cupholders. Not just for drinks, but also for phones and sunglasses, because it’s easier to remember them when they’re sticking up rather than hiding inside a console or lying on a charging pad. Note to designers: Can you do phone-charging cupholders?

The Ford Ranger that I had for the weekend got high marks for its back-seat cupholders and the fold-down console with its padded armrest. “I hate when there’s nowhere to lean,” declared the 16-year-old, weary already. It’s exhausting being so cool.

See all results for used cars for sale near 90017

Complaints about the Ranger were the lack of dedicated climate vents for the back seat—their mother and I were freezing up front while they were still too warm in the back—and the hefty step-up into the cabin, which was challenging while carrying a large milk tea with mango jelly. “I can’t hold the door and jump down at the same time,” the younger one said, exhibiting a remarkable concern for parking-lot door dings rarely shown by people who don’t pay insurance premiums (and often not even by those who do).

A later ride in my ’80s Dodge Ramcharger had the same step-up and A/C problems, but the couchlike bench seat and full-length windows offset the lack of modern amenities. That vehicle is a favorite of my neighbor’s kids too, so if you’re investing in collector cars, grab a Ramcharger or a Plymouth Trail Duster before they go the way of vintage Broncos in price.

While trucks from that era might seem like a potential family travel solution, not everyone is willing to let their kids ride in a vehicle where the closest things to airbags are the Gabriel HiJackers someone installed in 1973 and the whoopee cushion that’s been under the seat since two owners ago. With that in mind, I made a list of some modern back-seat luxuries that have impressed me or my discerning young friends in recent new cars.

Dual Bluetooth connectivity: Unless you like being critiqued for your playlist, the ideal setup lets a back-seat passenger connect their phone for media while the driver links theirs for ­navigation­ and­ communication.­ I ­first ­noticed­ this ­feature ­on a Dodge Durango, but it’s increasingly common in newer infotainment systems.

See all results for used cars for sale near 90017

Third-row USB ports: It’s pretty much a given that second rows will have charging points, but with bigger families, that middle row is where the child safety seats latch in. While little ones might run low on apple juice, the older crew in the wayback will require a different kind of recharge. If you’re looking at base models, make sure to check the USB-port placement, as those are the trims most likely to cheap out on the third row.

Smart seats: I recently drove the new long-wheelbase Tesla Model Y L­ and­ noted that ­the­ middle-row­ chairs­ fold­ flat­ but­ don’t ­slide.­ While­ I wasn’t thrilled about having to make my way down the center aisle, airplane style, to get to the third row, I was impressed that the seatbacks in the second and third rows can be raised or lowered from the driver’s seat. Another neat trick is that if the front passenger’s seat is left too far back, it can adjust itself to prevent the middle row from crashing into it during folding. Also, the middle-row armrests automatically lowered when the door opened to make entry to the back easier. This is what I want from technology: smart seats. Now give me back a physical shifter.

Sparkle: At the end of the weekend, I asked the 11-year-old her thoughts on the Ranger. “It’s okay,” she said. “Only I like those cars where the tables fold out of the seats and the ceiling is all sparkly, like those caves where there are stars in the cave.” Rolls-Royces? I also like those cars. I respect her outlook, so I didn’t tell her what those cave stars actually are. Note to designers: Have you considered glowworms?

Like a sleeper agent activated late in the game, Elana Scherr didn’t know her calling at a young age. Like many girls, she planned to be a vet-astronaut-artist, and came closest to that last one by attending UCLA art school. She painted images of cars, but did not own one. Elana reluctantly got a driver’s license at age 21 and discovered that she not only loved cars and wanted to drive them, but that other people loved cars and wanted to read about them, which meant somebody had to write about them. Since receiving activation codes, Elana has written for numerous car magazines and websites, covering classics, car culture, technology, motorsports, and new-car reviews. In 2020, she received a Best Feature award from the Motor Press Guild for the C/D story "A Drive through Classic Americana in a Polestar 2."  In 2023, her Car and Driver feature story "In Washington, D.C.'s Secret Carpool Cabal, It's a Daily Slug Fest" was awarded 1st place in the 16th Annual National Arts & Entertainment Journalism Awards by the Los Angeles Press Club.

Rams activate Myles Garrett, put Jaylen Watson on IR

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Myles Garrett is officially back on the Rams’ active roster.

The Rams announced on Saturday that they have activated Garrett from injured reserve. Head coach Sean McVay had already confirmed that the linebacker will play for the first time since Week 1 and the roster move clears the way for him to take the field against the Commanders on Sunday.

Running back Ronnie Rivers will also be available after being activated from injured reserve. Tight end Terrance Ferguson was expected back as well, but was scratched on Friday with a hamstring injury.

Cornerback Jaylen Watson will not play as he suffered a season-ending shoulder injury. He was placed on injured reserve Saturday and the Rams also elevated long snapper Jake McQuaide and cornerback Damarion Williams from the practice squad.

All 8 Episodes Of Netflix's R-Rated Thriller Miniseries Deliver Peak Psychological Horror

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Some of the greatest thrillers carefully transcend the screen and invade a viewer's personal space. They introduce audiences to such bizarre yet intimate scenarios that it becomes hard not to wonder if their hard-hitting terrors could someday happen in the real world. One of Netflix's better psychological thrillers is driven by the same principle, allowing it to stay with one long after the credits stop rolling.

Written by Son Ho-young, The Frog is relatively obscure compared to most mainstream thriller shows on Netflix. It follows a relatively simple premise where a mysterious visitor enters the lives of ordinary unsuspecting people before unleashing complete chaos. However, what starts as a simple psychological thriller soon turns into a tale of extreme obsession and manipulation.

The Frog's early appeal comes from its portrayal of quaint rural settings. It primarily unfolds in the brightness of the summer in seemingly safe and peaceful environments. This, however, is cleverly juxtaposed by the gripping Netflix K-drama's portrayal of how one can fatefully find darkness even during the brightness of the day and the peace of the night.

There is a famous Korean proverb that goes something like: "A stone thrown carelessly can kill a frog." The proverb is where the show gets its name from as it serves as the primary driver of its story. More often than not, in typical psychological thrillers, a game of cat-and-mouse unfolds between a protagonist and an antagonist.

The protagonist usually makes a mistake or lapse in judgment, which triggers an antagonist to chase them.

In Netflix's The Frog, however, characters do nothing to provoke "monsters." They are portrayed as the proverbial "frogs" who merely become targets and victims due to bad timing. By showing how one literally has to do nothing to attract trouble, The Frog instills a kind of existential dread that most psychological thrillers lack.

Instead of merely unfolding a thrilling game of cat-and-mouse, The Frog also uses one's denial and remorse as a narrative device. It shows how one does not merely become a "frog" when directly confronted by a villain. Even the agonizing struggle to overcome denial and accept misfortune can turn an individual into a "frog," trapped in the crossfire of someone else's wrongdoings.

The Netflix psychological thriller succeeds because it remains less fixated on a conventional battle between good and evil. Its primary emphasis remains on the psychological paralysis experienced by an innocent bystander trying to pretend nothing happened. With its more grounded portrayal of psychological horror, The Frog completely immerses viewers into its drama.

Even the show's characters seem so realistic and ordinary that it becomes hard not to see yourself in their place.

There is a sense of passiveness to The Frog's exploration of suspense and high-stakes psychological terror because of how it captures the consequences of inaction rather than blatant immorality. However, owing to its unique approach to the genre, The Frog risks feeling a little too uneven and detached from its own core story at times.

For the most part of its run, however, the Netflix thriller does a great job of making up for its flaws with its compelling character beats, brilliant performances, and incredible atmospheric contrast. Not to mention, its brief 8-episode runtime makes it perfectly bingeable.

Citizen reveals limited-edition Doctor Doom watch: Price, specs, and release date

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

TL;DR: Citizen has launched a limited-edition Marvel Avengers Dr. Doom watch, available to purchase now for $495.

We're almost overwhelmed by the tidal wave of Avengers: Doomsday brand collabs ahead of the movie's Dec. 18 release, but Citizen has just dropped something considerably more refined than novelty fast-food cups.

The watchmaker has officially unveiled the Marvel | Citizen Limited-Edition Avengers: Doomsday Timepiece, paying direct homage to Victor von Doom. Limited to just 3,100 pieces globally, the timepiece eschews cartoonish comic prints in favor of a textured, industrial aesthetic. It's nerdy and sexy all rolled into one.

It has a 43mm gray ion-plated case, gold-tone hour markers, an olive-green leather strap, and Citizen’s signature Eco-Drive quartz movement.

The watch runs on Citizen’s Eco-Drive light-powered movement. It pulls continuous operating charge from any ambient light source — including dull fluorescent home-office bulbs and desk lamps — meaning you will never have to crack the case open to replace anything. Nobody has time for that.

Each of the 3,100 units ships with custom villain-themed display packaging, an etched caseback, and a heavy, double-sided Doctor Doom collectible challenge coin. It's an absolute dream for collectors.

7 Great Netflix Shows You Can Only Watch Once

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

These seven Netflix shows are some of the best the streamer has to offer, but you'll probably only ever watch them once. There's a long history of TV shows that you would only ever want to watch once. Series that either don't have the same power on a second watch, or perhaps have a bit too much power.

Some series are reliant on a twist that loses its "oomph" once you know it. Some are dark referendums on real history that can be painful to acknowledge. Some just don't hold up as well the second time around. Whatever the reason, these seven shows are absolutely worth watching once, but just not twice.

BoJack Horseman is an adult animated comedy-drama that goes from mostly comedy with a sprinkling of drama at the beginning of its six seasons, to primarily a heavy drama with some dashes of comedy. The series stars the titular BoJack, an anthropomorphic (one of many in the world of the show) horse.

A former sitcom star, BoJack's life has been on a downward trajectory for years, exacerbated by his drinking, depression, and philandering. Now a washed-up former star, BoJack attempts to get his life back on track but keeps getting in his own way. Across six seasons, BoJack seems to break more relationships than he mends.

It's a very funny and clever series, and extremely relatable for anyone who has found themselves regretting their past actions. BoJack Horseman only gets darker as the seasons go on, and episodes about Alzheimer's, abuse, and self-harm become the norm. It's a great watch, but not one you'll quickly return to.

American Vandal is a highly underrated mockumentary series that parodied the popular true-crime documentaries of the era like Making a Murderer and Serial, the first of which premiered on Netflix. The two-season series is an anthology, and in each, a mundane crime or prank is handled like a notorious murder case.

In season 1, a prank leaves 27 faculty cars covered in graphic graffiti. In season 2, a cafeteria's lemonade is contaminated with maltitol. Both seasons utilize the same dramatic filmmaking, talking heads, closed-circuit camera footage, and foreboding storytelling of the true-crime TV genre.

Back when American Vandal came out, true crime was at the height of its popularity. Eight years later, the tropes that the show is poking fun at are a little less popular and relevant. This was a great series when it came out, but like a lot of satire, it loses its bite as the rest of the culture catches up.

Mike Flanagan's horror TV shows are all worthy of your time, though don't go into any of them expecting a rip-roaring horror filled with jump scares. His series are slow and thoughtful, quietly building dreadful atmospheres that make the few jump scares there are all the more impactful.

However, this does mean they can be a bit difficult to return to. Once you know what the shows are building towards, the slower parts get a bit more tedious. Even the best of Flanagan's miniseries, The Haunting of Hill House, suffers from this issue. It's a series dripping with atmosphere, horror, and sadness, and that makes returning to it difficult.

The miniseries is based on Shirley Jackson's story of the same name, though it plays with the structure of the story and adds an additional timeline that the show jumps between. It's a beautifully written plot, and Flanagan builds up some shockingly scary sequences all while keeping everything in the winding tale intelligible.

When They See Us is a four-episode true crime miniseries about the Central Park Five. In 1989, in Central Park in Manhattan, five young Black and Latino males were accused of raping and assaulting a white woman who was jogging in the park that evening. Despite little evidence, the five young men were charged and sentenced to prison for anywhere between seven and 13 years.

In 2002, DNA evidence exonerated the five men, and their convictions were vacated and they were released. The series begins with the events leading up to the assault; the police investigation into Kevin Richardson (Asante Blackk), Antron McCray (Caleel Harris), Yusef Salaam (Ethan Herisse), Korey Wise (Jharrel Jerome), and Raymond Santana (Marquis Rodriguez); and goes through their imprisonments and eventual releases.

When They See Us is a harrowing, brutal examination of the failures of the U.S. justice system, and the systemic racism that will feel all too relatable even to those living in 2026. It's a hard watch, one that forces the viewer to confront the realities about the inequalities we live with every day.

1899 is a multilingual German series from the creators of Dark, so you can guess that this historical drama is going to go in some weird directions. The show is set in the titular year and follows the passengers of the steamship Kerberos, bound from Southampton in the UK to New York City.

Throughout TV history, networks and streaming services alike have produced various great miniseries, but only some are perfect from start to finish.

On their journey, the Kerberos gets a message indicating its sister ship, Prometheus, has not sunk, as previously thought, and is stranded somewhere in the Atlantic. Upon meeting the survivors, the travelers on Kerberos begin to suspect that something very strange and very dangerous is going on.

1899 had the makings of a thrilling, mind-bending sci-fi series along the same lines as Dark, but the series was canceled after just one season. There's now basically no reason to ever rewatch the show, considering the ending in particular, which sets up many seasons' worth of plotting.

Archive 81 is a Lovecraftian TV show, with obvious influences from the master of cosmic horror. The series is based on a podcast of the same name, and takes place across two timelines. The first is set in 1994 and follows Melody Pendras' (Dina Shihabi) attempts to document the story behind an apartment building fire.

The second storyline takes place in 2022 and follows archivist Dan Turner (Mamoudou Athie), who is asked to restore Melody's videos by a mysterious organization. There are cults, creatures, secrets, and dark gods, and Archive 81 manages to weave them all together without losing the plot or the frightening atmosphere.

For most of the series, the viewers are unsure of how much of a Lovecraft show Archive 81 is, and that mystery keeps things interesting. However, towards the end of the series, all the mystery falls away. Once you know where Archive 81 is going, it loses a bit of its mystique, making rewatches less interesting.

Baby Reindeer is one of the best Netflix originals the streamer has ever put out. The seven-episode miniseries was created by and stars Richard Gadd, based on his one-man play, which was in turn based upon his real-life experiences. In the show, Gadd changes his character's name to Donny Dunn.

Donny is an aspiring stand-up comedian who makes the mistake of befriending one Martha Scott (Jessica Gunning), a former lawyer with a criminal record. The lonely and unstable Martha becomes obsessed with Donny, ruining his relationships and forcing him to confront an abusive episode in his past.

It is a fantastic series, in no small part due to Gadd's personal connection to the story. However, it can be a very difficult watch, particularly when it comes to the scenes of sexual assault, which are critical to Baby Reindeer. It's a Netflix series everyone should watch once, and follow up with a long, deep breath.

Larry Gagosian Documentary Director Says He Isn’t Trying to Cancel Famed Art Dealer

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Filmmaker Barry Avrich said the unauthorized doc is an exposé but will also be a balanced look at the go-to art dealer.

Documentary filmmaker Barry Avrich has turned his gaze on famed New York and Los Angeles art dealer Larry Gagosian for his next project.

But while some headlines have painted the unauthorized documentary, entitled Shadow Man: Inside the Secret World of Larry Gagosian, as a “cancellation” of Gagosian, Avrich said he aims to be balanced as he uncovers how Gagosian became arguably the most important art dealer for the past several decades. The 81-year-old owns several galleries across New York City and in Beverly Hills, London, Paris and more, and represents hundreds of the most-coveted artists, making him the go-to dealer for celebrities and Hollywood executives.

“Is it an exposé? Yes. Are there things in there, or comments and opinions that he won’t like? Sure. But it will be a very balanced biography of this person,” Avrich told The Hollywood Reporter. “There’ll be stuff he likes. There’ll be stuff that he detests. But that’s a documentary. I’m not doing a Michael Moore piece here.”

This will be Avrich’s third film about the art world after Made You Look, about an art forgery case and a Netflix film, Blurred Lines, which looked at the commercial forces in the art world, in addition to docs about producer Lew Wasserman and Vanity Fair columnist Dominick Dunne. Throughout the process of filming both art films, he was inspired to create this documentary.

“Larry’s name just kept coming up because you just can’t ignore it. When someone owns a lane, or invents a better mousetrap, whether it’s a Jeff Bezos or a Steve Jobs or an Elon Musk with Tesla, you just can’t ignore the sheer power and domination of an industry,” Avrich said. “And I’m fascinated by him. I’m fascinated that as the world changes constantly, that art continues to be this extraordinarily coveted asset for many different reasons, and Larry Gagosian has seemed to be somewhat Teflon and maintained this amazing control of the industry.”

While he again emphasizes that it is not meant to be a hit piece, Avrich said the documentary will examine how he’s been able to get a corner on the comparing it to “opening up a car dealership and having all of the greatest brands under one roof, but preventing those brands from being available to any other dealership. “

So far, Avrich said he has been fielding many emails and phone calls from people who want to participate in the documentary, and plans to include collectors, artists and former staff that have worked with him.

Avrich said he’s not looking for approval from Gagosian, who he has yet to speak with, but would welcome him to participate in the documentary.

A press representative for Gagosian did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

Avrich and his team have completed their research and will soon be moving from pre-production into production, with plans to start filming in at the beginning of 2027, he said. He’s currently in conversations with two distributors, with plans to release in late 2027 or spring 2028 tied to a time “where I think the art world is watching,” he said.

Colts rule out CB Justin Walley, LB Akeem Davis-Gaither

The Colts ruled out cornerback Justin Walley (knee) and linebacker Akeem Davis-Gaither (knee) ahead of Sunday’s game against the Cowboys.

Walley injured his knee during the Week 7 game against the Vikings and has not practiced or played since. Davis-Gaither did not practice this week.

‘Zi’ Review: Kogonada Rediscovers His Voice with a Microbudget Hong Kong Experiment Starring Michelle Mao and Haley Lu Richardson

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Editor’s Note: This review was originally published during the 2026 Sundance Film Festival. Grasshopper Film releases “Zi” in select theaters starting Friday, November 6.

A Hong Kong violinist who’s losing her grip on reality, young Zi (Michelle Mao) is haunted by visions of her future self. The doctors at the local neurology center suspect that she might be suffering from the early symptoms of a brain tumor, but Zi — a being of pure cinema, cobbled together from the half-formed memories of films like “La Jetée” and “Cléo from 5 to 7” — will have to wait a long and lonely 24 hours for the test results to come back.  Related Stories ‘To Hold a Mountain’ Took on the Government — Now It’s Montenegro’s Oscar Submission ‘Soul Patrol’ Review: The Vietnam War’s First Black Special Ops Team Shares Their Stories in An Enlightening Documentary

Well, maybe not so lonely. Crying on one of the innumerable concrete steps that climb Lantau Island, Zi is consoled by a sweet American expat with a strangely familiar face (and an aggressively unnatural blonde wig). Her name is L, she’s a disillusioned former dancer played by the luminous Haley Lu Richardson, and she can’t help but reciprocate the inexplicable closeness that Zi feels towards her. Over the course of a single night together, these two lost souls will help to relocate each other in time — to resynchronize with the world around them, reconnect with the infinite potential of the present, and return themselves to the creative purpose that once anchored them to it.

Even if seen without a shred of context, it would be obvious to anyone that “Zi” was made by someone who is — or hopefully was — desperate to spark a realignment of his own. To steal himself away from regret and anxiety and tether his perspective back to the now. Shot without a script or a budget in the immediate aftermath of Kogonada’s ill-received “A Big Bold Beautiful Journey” (the “After Yang” auteur’s studio debut, by all indications a soul-crushing experience for an artist whose two previous features retained the delicate grace of the video essays that he used to make), this searching and unformed wisp of an experiment hardly represents the first time that an ascendent director has scaled down after a misstep, but “Zi” takes the notion of “one for me” to much greater and more galvanizing extremes than usual.

This isn’t just a filmmaker trying to get back on track, it’s a filmmaker desperately, nakedly searching for a way to fall back in love with filmmaking itself. A way to prove that it might still love him back. (Kogonada described the project to IndieWire as “A retreat, as well as a pursuit.”)

While the prospect of watching Kogonada do that will naturally be of greater interest to those who have watched him get to this point, this movie’s palpably improvised nature — its stolen shots and recursive shape lend the impression of someone trying to cut their ultra-evocative vacation footage into a coherent narrative — would force anyone to wonder about the meta-textual aspects of its creation. “You feel like your sole purpose in life is something, and it doesn’t choose you back,” L sputters out at one point. “What do you do with that? I had to get away from it all. That’s why I came here. Wandering.”

Since arriving in Hong Kong, she’s made a habit of collecting sounds in much the same way as Kogonada has come there on the hunt for images. They both find a dissonance around Victoria Harbor that reflects their internal feelings of otherness and self-alienation. It’s China, but not. It’s a single city, but one split across two main areas by a stretch of the sea that some people never cross. It’s among the world’s most densely populated metropolises, but the vertical terrain seems fundamentally inhospitable to human civilization, and nature continues to assert itself between every skyscraper, shopping mansion, and crack in the sidewalk. Zi is the only native character in the film, but the history and design of Hong Kong reflects her disassociation as well — like her, the former British colony’s experience of the present is delayed.

That’s the closest thing to a diagnosis we get for what ails her, though it’s clear that Kogonada is leaning more towards soft science-fiction than the stuff of hard medicine. “Temoral relativism” is how L’s ex-fiancé Min (Jin Ha) describes it, though he’s slow to reveal how much he knows about Zi’s condition. Everything she perceives is already in the past. I suppose that’s true for all of us to some degree, if you factor in the nanoseconds that elapse between stimuli and our ability to perceive it, but the situation is more pronounced for Zi (who Mao plays with the effervescent helplessness of a paper airplane). Of course, that doesn’t explain why she believes she’s bearing witness to visions of her future self, but that confusion only exacerbates her feelings of distance from the present.

Even in its more platonic dimensions, the threadbare story that gradually allows Zi to close that gap is awash with the romance of loss, reconciliation, and belonging. Kogonada is falling back in love with the movies right before our eyes, and yet,  in characteristic fashion, that process isn’t garish or forced. His is a vision that is revealed through careful subtraction — by clearing away all of the dust and smudges that came to obscure it.

While Kogonada indulges in a few legible winks to his cinematic forebears (the most obvious of those being a step-printed homage to Wong Kar Wai, and my most far-fetched being a disoriented nod to José Luis Guerín’s “In the City of Sylvia”), he and cinematographer Benjamin Loeb almost completely eschew the neon exoticism that foreigners tend to look for in Hong Kong, with the vast majority of the film being captured in unfussy handheld shots that accentuate its here-and-now anxiety and delegate the emotional heavy lifting to an impeccably curated soundtrack of Ryuichi Sakamoto remixes and originals.

By the end, “Zi” engenders a certain emotional attachment toward and between its characters, all of it building to a gently unexpected gutpunch of a final minute. But even that last-minute reveal — not a plot twist so much as a sudden change in perspective — is rooted more in the movie’s form than its plot, as Kogonada’s characters remain largely symbolic of their function in the writer-director’s journey back to himself.

It’s a testament to Mao, Ha, and especially Richardson — who continues to be so good at grounding Kogonada’s most abstract ideas in a tender human place — that “Zi” retains even a soft dramatic pull as it searches for the present, though its recursive nature certainly wasn’t conceived with a casual audience in mind. If you’re hooked, which I wasn’t, or haunted by it, which I was, that will likely have less to do with an acute emotional connection to these characters than with the overflowing rewards of watching someone rediscover the sound of their own voice, and hear a way forward into the future in its echoes.

“Zi” premiered at the 2026 Sundance Film Festival. Grasshopper Film releases it in select theaters starting Friday, November 6.

Hailee Steinfeld Reveals What She Didn’t Expect From Motherhood

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Hailee Steinfeld detailed what she’s learned about herself from the “transformative experience” of being a mom to her and Josh Allen’s baby girl Harper.

Hailee Steinfeld is learning to play new notes as a first-time mom.

Six months after she gave birth to baby girl Harper with husband Josh Allen, the Pitch Perfect 2 star shed light on the unexpected impact motherhood has had on her relationship with herself.

“There’s a long answer, but I’ll give the short one here: I’ve learned just how much of myself I still have to discover,” Steinfeld wrote the Nov. 3 edition of her Beau Society newsletter on Substack, in response to a fan question. “I thought becoming a mom would be this transformative experience about loving someone else, and it is that 1000 percent.”

“But what I didn’t expect is how much she’s teaching me about myself,” she added. “She’s made me more patient, more present, and so much more aware of how special the little things are.”

And as the Hexed actress continues to discover new aspects about herself amid her motherhood journey, she’s also learning to take her own advice.

“I’m really good at reminding people that they’re human, that things take time, and that everything happens for a reason,” Steinfeld wrote, “and it will all fall into place—how, when, and where it should—and that they don’t have to have everything all figured out.”

Yet, she admitted, “I’m not always the best at taking that advice myself.”

But since becoming a mom, the Sinners star—who married Allen in May 2025—has made sure that her own wellbeing is still a priority.

“This season of life has definitely taught me to slow down, really just soak in the small pockets of joy that are truly all around us,” she shared on the Nov. 5 episode of Live With Kelly and Mark. “Time for myself looks a little different now, but I find it.”

And this mindset of cherishing every moment is surely beneficial when it comes to wrapping her heard around how fast her daughter is growing up.

“I feel like she’s 6 months, and also 4 weeks, and also 5 years old,” Steinfeld told Kelly Ripa and Mark Consuelos. “Time is crazy how it works.”

“She is totally on the move,” she added. “This girl is crawling around.”

3 Best New Netflix Series To Binge-Watch This Weekend (November 6–8, 2026)

The top 3 new Netflix series to binge-watch this weekend, November 6–8, 2026, include a 3-part true crime documentary, a gripping historical drama, and a returning spy thriller hit. Netflix continues its stellar Fall 2026 streaming rollout with three fantastic new series worth your time this weekend, including the #1 most-watched series in the world that is currently dominating viewership charts. This new series dethroned past Netflix hits such as A Couple of Lies, Lupin, and The One About Matthew Perry, which are all still trending right now.

ScreenRant's expert recommendations for the best new shows to watch on Netflix this weekend round up the most critically acclaimed and popular series heading into the first weekend of November. Whether you're looking for an immersive, intense thriller based on true events or a star-studded spy series with sharply written dark comedic elements, it does not get any better than these three handpicked series. Make sure to check out our updated streaming guides for Prime Video, Paramount+, and HBO Max this weekend as well.

Taken: Natalee Holloway has officially taken the streaming world by storm by becoming Netflix's most-watched new series worldwide. Spanning just 3 episodes, this true crime docuseries explores the 2005 disappearance of an 18-year-old woman named Natalee Holloway who went missing during a graduation trip in Aruba. Taken: Natalee Holloway dives into Natalee's mother, Beth, and her relentless search to discover the truth about what happened to her daughter that spanned more than 20 years.

Under Siege is one of the best new Netflix series worth watching this weekend that has mostly flown under the radar ahead of its release on Friday, November 6. The historical thriller series chronicles the infamous 1985 hostage crisis that took place at the Palace of Justice in Colombia, portraying several of the key figures who were actually involved in the political attack. Under Siege primarily follows protagonist Pilar Navarrete (Natalia Reyes), a woman looking for her missing husband, Jimmy Beltrán (Camilo Amores), who worked at the Palace of Justice during the crisis.

The development process for Under Siege took more than seven years to complete in order to provide the most accurate portrayal of the events that have ever been dramatized in a television series. The production team interviewed dozens of actual people connected to the hostage situation, which lasted two days beginning on November 6, 1985, exactly 41 years before the Netflix series debuted. Under Siege also explores the aftermath of the crisis and the motivations of the attackers, the M-19 urban guerrilla group, which was allegedly funded directly by Pablo Escobar.

Black Doves season 2 just premiered on Netflix this Thursday, November 5, and is ScreenRant's top choice for what to binge-watch on the platform this weekend. Picking up where season 1 left off, Black Doves season 2 follows Keira Knightley's protagonist, Helen Webb, a member of the Black Doves secret society that carries out assassinations and acts of espionage for the highest bidder, regardless of nationalistic or ideological allegiances. Helen's tasked with continuing to spy on her politician husband, Wallace, who has just been appointed as Britain's new Prime Minister.

Renowned Filmmaker Carla Gutiérrez Joins Executive Producing Team On ICE-Themed Oscar Contender ‘Water Cooler’

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

EXCLUSIVE: Oscar-shortlisted filmmaker Carla Gutiérrez has signed on to executive produce the award-winning short documentary Water Cooler, joining a distinguished group of talents.

Along with Gutiérrez (director of Frida and editor of RBG), the ICE-themed film is executive produced by Oscar nominees Sam Pollard, Geeta Gandbhir, and Alisa Payne through their production company Message Pictures, and by Emmy winner Blair Foster. Emma V.F. directed Water Cooler, which won Best Documentary Short at the Palm Springs International ShortFest 2026, qualifying it for Academy Award consideration. It also won the Golden Starfish Award at the Hamptons International Film Festival. The short makes its international debut at IDFA in Amsterdam, the world’s largest documentary festival that opens next week. Related Stories Documentary Geeta Gandbhir, Sam Pollard, Alisa Payne Board ICE-Themed Oscar Contender 'Water Cooler' As EPs Documentary Oscar-Contending 'Frida' Team On Tracking Down Famed Mexican Artist's Treasures In Unexpected Places

The 12-minute short “depicts federal agents waiting to arrest their targets outside of courtrooms in New York’s Federal Plaza immigration court,” notes a synopsis. “The observational documentary — which she filmed from November 2025 through January 2026 — juxtaposes agents’ casual conversations with tense scenes of the agents arresting immigrants who attended required court hearings to seek legal status.” Watch on Deadline

In a statement, Gutiérrez said, “Immigrants around the world are being scapegoated as the source of all of society’s problems. This dehumanization gives way to detached violence and cruelty, as Water Cooler strikingly portrays.”

Water Cooler has been shortlisted for the Cinema Eye Honors and chosen for DOC NYC’s shortlist of the year’s top documentaries; it screens at the latter festival on November 13 and is likewise set to screen at City Lights Film Festival in London on Nov. 16.

“I’m thrilled to have Carla’s support as Water Cooler debuts on the international festival circuit,” Emma V.F. commented. “I hope the film helps inspire people to fight the rise of fascism around the world.”

‘To Hold a Mountain’ Took on the Government — Now It’s Montenegro’s Oscar Submission

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

For one of the most important screenings of “To Hold a Mountain,” the filmmakers had to bring their own electricity. In July, co-directors Biljana Tutorov and Petar Glomazić hauled a tent, a generator, and a DCP up to the Montenegrin highlands where they had spent years filming.

The creative couple, also partnered in life, wanted the shepherds who lived and worked in Sinjajevina to see the movie they had made possible. But by then, Montenegro had already resolved their story’s central conflict — scrapping the government’s plan to turn these people’s ancestral pastures into a military training ground after joining NATO in 2017. Related Stories ‘Soul Patrol’ Review: The Vietnam War’s First Black Special Ops Team Shares Their Stories in An Enlightening Documentary Could ‘The Further Mis-Adventures of Cliff Booth’ Re-Ignite Quentin Tarantino’s Bruce Lee Controversy?

“To Hold a Mountain” won the World Cinema Grand Jury Prize for documentary at Sundance, and Montenegro’s prime minister and president even showed up to the shepherds’ screening with their families, unannounced. When the film opens at New York’s Film Forum on December 16, American audiences will see the feature’s updated conclusion.

“We reopened the film to continue shooting and then a second end happened that was very empowering and powerful,” Tutorov said at IndieWire’s Art of the Doc series.

During our conversation, the filmmaker recalled arriving in Sinjajevina with Glomazić on the first day of protest. They wanted to help the community and make a movie, and agreed early on that those were two different jobs.

“We will separate our activist mission from our filmmaker’s mission,” she said. “That was a very good decision because it allowed us to be very bold in both roles.”

Onscreen, the fight over who would get to lay claim to the mountain centered on two people who already did: Gara, a shepherd and community leader, and Nada, the young girl she was raising. As the threat to their home brought a history of violence in their own family to the surface, the directors had to reckon with how much of Nada’s private life might become public.

“We really wanted it to be an empowering experience for [her],” Tutorov said.

Nada was twelve and a half when the filmmakers began to understand what the story would ask of her, and Tutorov said they decided to keep filming until she turned eighteen — precisely so Nada could decide for herself whether she wanted to be part of it. The crew logged more than 230 shooting days, though some yielded only half an hour of footage.

Gara and Nada had work to do, too. The directors and cinematographer Eva Kraljević shared a small shepherd’s shed, assisting with various chores including cows that had to be milked twice a day. Over successive summers, their routine became a framing device. Tutorov, who studied art history, thinks of the mountain as a kind of chorus bearing witness to living things on it.

Last July, just as the filmmakers were wrapping up, Montenegro’s government canceled the training-ground plan. Tutorov believes “To Hold a Mountain” and how it told the existing community’s story influenced that decision. She also pointed out that a different government was in power by 2025, following years of organizing by Sinjajevina locals.

At the shepherd’s screening a year later, Prime Minister Milojko Spajić said the film reinforced his view that canceling the plan had been the right call. Then Montenegro chose “To Hold a Mountain” as its official submission for the international feature Oscar. Tutorov said there was irony in realizing that, after years of fighting the government, she and Glomazić would be proudly representing the country on a global stage.

“It feels like they really kind of destroyed our strategy,” she said, warmly.

It’s a good joke, and an even better reason to remember that rough-and-tumble showing. For an evening, the people who’d spent years making their case to the government got to host its leaders on the very land they’d fought to keep. Tutorov recalled what Montenegro’s president told the filmmakers after seeing the movie: “Thank you guys for teaching us democracy.”

National Geographic Documentary Films, part of a joint venture between The Walt Disney Company and the National Geographic Society, is committed to bringing the world premium, feature documentaries that cover timely, provocative and globally relevant stories from the very best documentary filmmakers. Its award-winning and critically acclaimed films reach 300 million people worldwide in 180 countries and 33 languages across the global National Geographic channels and direct-to-consumer platforms Disney+ and Hulu.Recent films include the Emmy-nominated winner of the IDA Award for Best Feature “The Tale of Silyan;” Oscar® and Peabody nominee “Sugarcane;” Oscar-nominated and Peabody Award winner “Bobi Wine: The People’s President;” Emmy Award winner “Lost In The Jungle;” Oscar- and BAFTA-nominated “Fire of Love;” Emmy and Peabody Award winner “The Territory;” BAFTA nominees “The Rescue” and “Becoming Cousteau;” and Oscar- and BAFTA winner “Free Solo.” For more information visit films.nationalgeographic.com, or find us onFacebook,Twitter,Instagram, and YouTube.

A farmer in Mexico saw the ground split open in his cornfield, then watched a volcano grow to 1,391 feet tall

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

In 1943, Mexico's Parícutin volcano erupted from a farmer's cornfield, growing rapidly and burying two villages during nine years of activity.

On February 20, 1943, farmer Dionisio Pulido watched a fissure open in his field near the village of Parícutin, in the Mexican state of Michoacán. Smoke rose from the crack, along with a hissing sound and a smell of sulfur.

Within a week, the new cone stood more than 490 feet (150 meters) tall. When the eruption ended in 1952, it had risen 1,391 feet (424 meters) above the cornfield where it began.

Lava buried two villages along the way. Parícutin was the first volcano whose eruption modern scientists observed in detail from its birth, according to the International Union of Geological Sciences (IUGS).

Starting around February 5 to 7, 1943, local witnesses felt about two weeks of earthquake activity before the eruption.

Felipe Cuara Amezcua, then municipal president of San Juan Parangaricutiro, later reported that the tremors grew until more than 200 shook the area in a single day.

On February 20, Pulido went to his farm to prepare the fields for the spring sowing. He had owned the land for 31 years.

Smithsonian mineralogist William F. Foshag and Mexican geologist Jenaro González R. later collected his account and his wife’s. A 1956 U.S. Geological Survey bulletin printed them in English translation.

At about 4 p.m., Pulido left his wife, Paula, and went to burn a pile of branches. She had been hearing underground noise and thunder.

“About 4 p. m., after talking to my husband, I heard a kind of loud whistle,” Paula Pulido recalled.

“I saw that it was a kind of fissure that had only a depth of half a meter,” Pulido said.

He then saw the ground swell by about 6.6 to 8 feet (2 to 2.5 meters), while gray smoke or dust rose from the crack.

“Immediately more smoke began to rise, with a hiss or whistle, loud and continuous,” he recounted.

Frightened, he mounted his mare and galloped to the village where his family was waiting.

Accounts from Pulido and other witnesses gave “the first adequate account of the birth of a new volcano,” Foshag and González wrote.

One of the first photographs of the eruption, taken at 6 p.m. on February 20, shows an ash plume rising from the new vent. By midnight, the cone was about 20 feet (6 meters) high.

On the morning of February 21, a strong earthquake caused panic. People from Parícutin fled to San Juan Parangaricutiro, and many from San Juan went on to Angahuan or Uruapan.

A photograph from February 21 shows the cone about 100 feet (30 meters) tall, just rising above the treetops. After six days of activity, it reached 548 feet (167 meters).

The first lava flow, called the Quitzocho flow, moved north over cornfields prepared for planting. Mexican geologist Ezequiel Ordóñez estimated that the early lava advanced about 10 feet (3 meters) an hour.

Ash fell too. It piled up deeper than 6 inches (15 centimeters) over an area of 116 square miles (300 square kilometers) around the volcano.

When the first phase of activity ended on October 19, 1943, the original vent had built a cone about 1,200 feet (365 meters) high.

Pulido’s home village lay only 1.9 miles (3 kilometers) northwest of the new cone. Its 733 residents had to abandon it for good four months after the eruption began, and lava later flowed over the site.

Starting in January 1944, a new phase sent out the Taquí lava flows, which spread over about 7 square miles (18 square kilometers) by 1945.

In July 1944, lava surrounded the church of San Juan Parangaricutiro. Its tower still stands in the middle of a lava flow, the IUGS notes.

In all, the volcano’s lavas reached up to 2.5 miles (4 kilometers) from the vent and formed a field of about 9.7 square miles (25 square kilometers).

The Mexican government gave displaced residents new land in Caltzontzín, 17 miles (27 kilometers) to the southeast.

Parícutin was “the first well-documented example of an eruption of a monogenetic volcanic cone in historical time,” according to a 1993 USGS film report.

A monogenetic cone forms during a single eruptive episode. Parícutin, active for about nine years, gave scientists an unprecedented chance to follow such a volcano through its full life cycle.

An international group documented the activity in real time. Celedonio Gutiérrez, a resident of San Juan who kept a diary from the first day, worked with U.S. scientists throughout the eruption.

In 1990, volcanologists I. Yokoyama and S. de la Cruz-Reyna analyzed the precursory earthquakes of the 1943 eruption, a type of signal that comes before eruptions.

In 1985, decades after the eruption stopped, a fumarole at Parícutin still measured 883°F (473°C). The cone is one of more than 1,400 vents in the Michoacán-Guanajuato volcanic field.

The information was obtained from the Smithsonian Institution’s Global Volcanism Program.

'Breaking Bad' Dean Norris Fights To Save Marriage, Travels With Estranged Wife Amid Divorce

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Dean Norris and his wife, Bridget, aren't throwing in the towel on their marriage ... and the "Breaking Bad" star says their struggling restaurant is already back on track.

The couple was spotted out together at LAX on Friday, and they were all smiles despite our recent reporting about their divorce and financial troubles ... and Dean seemed optimistic when asked about both.

Dean told photographers the pair were doing "great" and heading to Washington, D.C., for a Veterans Affairs event ... before declaring their restaurant, Swing Inn Cafe, is "already saved."

And when asked if he's hoping to save his marriage, Dean didn't hesitate ... responding, "Of course."

As TMZ first reported ... Bridget filed for divorce in 2025 after more than 23 years of marriage, citing irreconcilable differences. The couple's Temecula restaurant also filed for Chapter 11 bankruptcy in October, listing more than $1.8 million in liabilities against roughly $147,000 in assets.

The couple has also listed their sprawling Temecula estate for nearly $3.2 million ... adding to the recent headlines surrounding their finances.

It's kinda shocking to see them together here ... and judging by their smiles at the airport, Dean and Bridget aren't letting the drama stop them from traveling together ... or potentially working things out.

The Remarried Empress Cast Reveal Their Characters' Inner Lives

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The Remarried Empress is already making a splash during its premiere week, establishing itself as Disney+'s number-one Korean title in over 50 markets in the three days after its launch. Set in the imaginary world of the Eastern Empire, the K-drama is based on a globally renowned webtoon of the same name, following Empress Navier (Shin Min-a) as she picks up the pieces of her broken marriage and comes out on top.

When Emperor Sovieshu (Ju Ji-hoon) returns from a hunting trip with a runaway slave he aims to make his mistress, Navier must take stock of her role as wife and co-ruler of her beloved country. While she and her competition, Rashta (Lee Se-young), size each other up, the arrival of Prince Heinrey (Lee Jong-suk) of the Western Kingdom complicates things even further. Soon enough, Navier is swept up in the mystery, magic, and romance of the rival country's royal.

ScreenRant interviewed the cast of The Remarried Empress, asking them to share the vulnerable sides of Navier and Sovieshu, the bird-like style of Heinrey, and the intentions of Rashtha. The actors also revealed their personal favorite sets and costumes while filming on location in places like the Czech Republic and Germany.

ScreenRant: Min-a, you mentioned the drama shows a different side of Navier. What was one of the first moments in the script where you really connected with her humanity?

Shin Min-a: I think that, as an empress, Navier is trained to hold back from expressing her emotions right away. In that respect, as an actor, I've always had my own ways of restraining myself when I feel certain emotions. When Navier is upset, she shares what's on her mind with Queen in a measured and appropriate way, while I similarly open up to close friends.

When I first read the script, I felt that she showed so little emotion, and the weight she carried as an empress felt a bit stifling. But at the same time, I thought, “We might actually have something in common.” The way she handles and expresses her feelings is also something I admire and would like to emulate.

ScreenRant: Ji-hoon, similarly, you mentioned the importance of perspective. What’s an early moment in the script where you felt bad for Sovieshu or really understood his reasoning - as opposed to seeing him as a villain?

Ju Ji-hoon: What I understand is that anyone can have ambivalent feelings. He feels guilt toward the woman he loves, but also a sense of duty to have a child and continue the royal line.

And even though it begins as duty, there's also the genuine attraction he must have felt toward Rashta, as one person to another. Those feelings may be taboo in our society, but if no one were to say, “You can't do that,” they're feelings that could naturally arise. So, I focused on trying to understand that.

ScreenRant: Jong-suk, you mentioned the CGI team incorporated elements of your look into Queen’s design. What bird-like elements did you bring to your performance?

Lee Jong-suk: Actually, the order was that I came first, and then the bird was created. So, I think the CG team gave it much more thought than I did. On set, we had a lot of conversations along the lines of, “If you could emphasize certain characteristics a little more, it would help us create the bird in CG.”

ScreenRant: Se-young, fans have obsessively discussed the confrontation between Rashta and Navier in the trailer. Do you think Rashta’s trajectory would be different if Navier had welcomed her more warmly?

Lee Se-young: I don't think it has a big influence. As her life in the imperial palace goes on, Rashta learns and gradually develops her own motives. But she's not the type to start walking on eggshells because of that one rejection.

ScreenRant: It’s not every day you film in the Czech Republic, with an imperial palace as your setting. Did each of you have a favorite set or costume in particular?

Ju Ji-hoon: For me, it was the grand castles where we did our first shoot in the Czech Republic. Not just one castle, but several. We had them entirely to ourselves, without any tourists – that's a moment you rarely get in life. The moment I stepped into spaces that carried the real weight of time, I felt an energy I can't quite describe. Those spaces helped me a great deal in understanding and focusing on the story.

Shin Min-a: Filming scenes in the castles and gardens of the Czech Republic and Germany, I could feel a historical energy. Because we were entering a world we had created ourselves, we as actors had to be the first to fully step into it. The locations and costumes helped tremendously with that.

Lee Jong-suk: Our series did not require historical accuracy based on a specific era or country. As Min-a said, it's our own fantasy world, so I think we were able to create it through the feelings the spaces gave us, our co-stars' performances, and the entire crew working together as one.

Lee Se-young: I didn't change outfits that often, but I wore many very glamorous dresses. The one that stands out most is the dress from the scene where Rashta bids farewell to Sovieshu as he leaves, asking him not to forget her. When I tried it on during the fitting, I felt, “I've really become Rashta.” That was the first time I felt truly close to her.

New episodes of The Remarried Empress drop Wednesdays on Disney+ and Hulu in the U.S.

Bear Grylls Bought a ‘Derelict’ Island With ‘No Power’ and ‘Rats’ at 25 Instead of a London Apartment—Here’s How It Looks Decades Later

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Bear Grylls made an unusual decision decades ago, when he acquired a marital starter home with his wife, Shara Grylls.

“At 25, just married, we bought a small, abandoned, off-grid island from an ad in the back of a magazine,” the British adventurer, 52, wrote in a Nov. 3 Instagram caption.

Grylls revealed that the purchase of St. Tudwal’s Island West cost them around $120,000.

“There were many who thought us crazy,” he continued. “The best adventures start with nay-sayers. Thank you Shara for being brave and saying yes!”

While the comment section had users divided over the relatability of purchasing an entire island—with one commenter wondering how “celebrities get so far out of touch from reality and regular people”—there were also a handful of people who stepped in to point out that even when adjusted for inflation, the island was less costly than the average U.S. home.

The “Running Wild With Bear Grylls” star explained the decision to the Sunday Times in 2021, noting that snapping up a Welsh island seemed like a more exciting use of the couple’s money.

“In 2001, we were going to buy a London flat for $288,000, but we bought a houseboat on the Thames [now sold] and the island together, and it cost less than the apartment. So we decided on the path less trodden and an amazing adventure,” he said.

Grylls shed some light on just how dilapidated the property was 25 years ago.

“The island had a 200-year-old lighthouse and two lightkeeper’s cottages, but [the houses] were derelict with no power, no water, no roof, and rats,” he said. “I remember my first bath sitting in 3 inches of water pumped from the old tanks we’d cleaned out. There was rubble everywhere and rain coming through, but it was a great moment.”

Considering Grylls has made a career out of his ability to survive in extremely harsh conditions, it’s not entirely surprising that he was able to get through a few rough years before the island would become a more suitable dwelling.

It took a decade to get permission to build a slipway, which Grylls said they finished just a few years ago.

“It has been life-changing for us,” he said of the addition. “Previously we had to keep a boat on a mooring, row ashore, and walk a mile up the beach with all the shopping.”

Eventually, the two lightkeeper’s cottages were combined into a single home with four bedrooms. But the island is still off-grid.

“It’s 20 acres and 5 miles offshore with no mains, electricity, or running water,” Grylls told Vanity Fair in 2012. “It has a little lighthouse beside our house and is surrounded by amazing sea cliffs, seals, and dolphins. It is my favorite place on the planet!”

To travel to the destination, Grylls and his family take a “30-foot, high-speed ex-lifeboat,” which he described as a “rigid inflatable boat with twin 300-horsepower engines behind it that can tackle any sea it needs to.”

He said the house is “as tough as you get.” Thanks to “2-foot-thick old limestone walls,” it retains heat in the winter and stays cool in the summer.

“But when those gales come in, you can still feel it almost shake,” Grylls admitted. “Sea spray reaches the windows despite the 150-foot elevation.”

While St. Tudwal’s may lack some of the modern amenities guests might typically expect, the Grylls family does “sometimes have friends” visit.

“They need to be up for an adventure and prepared to get stuck there if the weather turns,” he said.

Grylls has recently made headlines for his connection to disgraced former comic Russell Brand, who was recently found guilty of one count of rape and three counts of sexual assault.

Grylls helped baptize Brand in a ceremony in the River Thames in 2024, after the allegations against the actor had come to light. Prior to the baptism, Brand appeared in a 2023 episode of Grylls’ show, in which the pair traveled across the Hebrides Islands.

“Brand’s faith seemed genuine to me at the time when I was a guest at his private baptism,” Grylls wrote in a Nov. 6 X post. “I have attended a few now. Even visiting in prisons with people who have committed terrible crimes. It’s part of our calling as men and women of faith, to love and serve anyone. It is the sick that need healing.”

Noting that “judgment is for the courts and God,” Grylls added that “it is right that justice has now been served.

“I can’t imagine how hard this time has been for the victims, the heroes of the day,” he said.

She has been writing entertainment and lifestyle content since 2016, with her work appearing in Cosmopolitan, BravoTV, NYLON, Bustle, Elite Daily, and The Zoe Report, among other publications. Hannah received a bachelor’s degree in journalism from The University of Maine. In her spare time, she loves to chat about pop culture and is a major fan of reality TV.

3 Coziest Movies to Watch on Netflix This Weekend (November 7-8)

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

We're late halfway through the Fall season, and with Halloween behind us, it's time to look ahead at what's coming. Right now, we're at that weird, in-between time before the winter holidays take over our cultural landscape. Normally, these few weeks are the perfect time to bask in the homey feeling of Fall, and there's no better way to do that than with a great, cozy movie.

When one thinks of cozy movies, usually genres like mystery, romantic comedy, fantasy, or animation come to mind. Luckily for you, Netflix has at least one major movie in each of those genres that would be the perfect choice for you to enjoy on a weekend at home. Here, we offer you the three best cozy movies you can watch on Netflix this weekend, ranging from a colorful and delightful animated adventure to a good, old-fashioned whodunit.

Let's start with a delightful mix of rom-com, mystery, and historical drama that deserves far more credit than it usually gets. Lily James stars in The Guernsey Literary and Potato Peel Pie Society as Juliet Ashton, a writer who travels to the island of Guernsey, which endured a harsh German occupation during World War II. There, she learns about the titular society and uncovers a mystery about the society's founder.

Benefitting from an incredibly charming ensemble cast that also includes Michiel Huisman, Penelope Wilton, Tom Courtenay, and Glen Powell, the film rises on the back of an intimate, heartfelt screenplay and Mike Newell's deft direction. James is winning as the lead, sharing sweet chemistry with Huisman and driving the narrative with the perfect balance of spunky determination and genuine emotion. There's a real sense of romantic warmth in this small but quirky island that makes the movie all the more entertaining and easy to revisit. Come for the romance and stay for the actually intriguing mystery and the sense of community between the oddball islanders.

What can possibly be cozier than a Wallace and Gromit adventure? Wallace & Gromit: Vengeance Most Fowl sees the intelligent and reliable dog Gromit growing concerned that his well-natured owner, Wallace (Ben Whitehead), has become overly dependent on the robotic garden gnome Norbot. Things take a turn when the criminal penguin mastermind Feathers McGraw steals Norbot to frame Wallace for a series of crimes, including the theft of the iconic Blue Diamond.

The film has a 100% rating on Rotten Tomatoes, and that perfect score is more than deserved. Wallace & Gromit: Vengeance Most Fowl is an enchanting claymation adventure that soars on the back of the dynamic between the two titular and universally beloved characters: one scene with Wallace and Gromit is more fun, nuanced, and lovely than most other movies out there. There's a lot of creativity on display, from the charming visuals to the clever, dry, incredibly British humor. The plot moves at a fast pace, the jokes land without fail, and the warm tone makes the whole thing endlessly enjoyable. It's one of those movies that feels like a literal warm hug, one that won't fail at bringing a smile to your face.

Any movie led by the talents of Celia Imrie, Sir Ben Kingsley, Dame Helen Mirren, and Pierce Brosnan is one that's worth seeing, especially if they're playing amateur sleuths. That's exactly what The Thursday Murder Club is about, based on the eponymous novel. The plot centers on four retirees at an English retirement village who enjoy theorizing about cold cases. They get their chance to level up when a murder happens right under their noses.

Stories about amateur sleuths proving their talent for actual crime-solving are a dime a dozen, but rarely are they more delightful. The Thursday Murder Club is a love letter to the whodunit genre with a nostalgic approach that makes it perfect for a quiet afternoon in. The plot is engaging but not particularly demanding, and the four incredible actors at the center are having the time of their lives. The result is a classic British movie that perfectly balances mystery with gentle humor and plenty of emotional moments. It doesn't really have many thrills, but honestly, it doesn't need them: in fact, the film is at its best when its four legendary protagonists are engaging with each other.
